# Preface.
## **God-beloved Reader,**

->I.<-

<image-desc>A square decorative woodcut initial 'D' depicting a landscape with rocky hills and a bird perched on a high peak.</image-desc>Holy Scripture, which is the foundation of our faith, contains not only many glorious sayings, but also very many remarkable histories.

II. To understand these, the science of topography is very useful; therefore, it is to be wished that all readers might have some knowledge <note>original: "cognition"</note> both of Palestine and the city of Jerusalem, as well as particularly of the situation of the Temple, because this contributes greatly to a clearer understanding of the entire Old Testament worship and its <term>Levitical ceremonies: the religious rituals and laws performed by the tribe of Levi as described in the first five books of the Bible</term>.

III. The descriptions and the copperplate engravings found in various authors <note>original: "Autoribus"</note>—provided they are true to the facts—are also a good aid for forming a clear mental image of these places and regions. However, not a single one of them provides such a distinct and vivid idea as when one sees the entire Temple standing before their eyes in a physical representation and model.

IV. Through this, not only do many passages that mention the Temple—of which one previously had only a dim understanding <note>original: "Concept"</note>—become bright and clear, but the circumstances of the histories that occurred in the Temple also present themselves and impress themselves so deeply upon the mind that one, as if an eyewitness present on the scene <note>original: "tanquam præsens spectator"</note>, is all the more fully convinced of the truth of the events that took place there <note>original: "de veritate rerum ibi peractarum convinciret"</note>.

<sig>A</sig>
<meta>catchword: V. The</meta>

<meta>This page continues the preface, specifically detailing the motivation for creating a physical model of the Temple of Solomon and the historical encounter with a Jewish rabbi's wax model that served as a catalyst.</meta>

->Preface.<-

V. This highly beneficial purpose—namely, the explanation of many sayings and histories in Holy Scripture—was the primary motive <note>original Latin: *primum movens*</note> for the effort to present <note>original: *sistiren*</note> the Temple of Solomon with all its courtyards, walls, gates, the Holy Place and the Holy of Holies, as well as all the <term>sacred vessels<gloss>original Latin: *vasis sacris*; the ritual objects used in Temple worship</gloss></term>. This was done according to the descriptions of the most famous authors in an actual model on a table five ells <note>An "ell" (German: *Elle*) was a traditional unit of measurement, roughly the length of a man’s forearm, approximately 24 inches.</note> long and nearly five ells wide.

VI. Just as the all-good God has bestowed His divine grace so that this small structure, with all the pieces described <note>original: *designirten*</note> in the following pages, could be brought into proper order and successfully completed.

VII. We have endeavored to seek the foundation of the entire Temple in the same place where <term>Lundius<gloss>Johann Lundius (1638–1686), a German pastor and author of a famous scholarly work on the Jewish Temple</gloss></term> found it. We have diligently consulted the authors he cited in Book II, Chapter IV, Section 12, so that no column or any part of the building would be placed contrary to the accounts of trustworthy writers <note>original Latin: *Scriptorum*</note>.

VIII. The first occasion for this construction was provided by a Jewish Rabbi from Prague, who in August 1716 brought a model of Solomon’s Temple made of white wax—one and a half ells long and wide—to Halle, and allowed anyone who desired to see it publicly. He was a man of good knowledge regarding the Jewish Law and their Temple, for whose rebuilding the Jews hope as longingly as we Christians hope for eternal life.

IX. However, the model he made with his own hand showed that he was not particularly experienced in mechanics <note>original Latin: *mechanicis*</note> and had little knowledge <note>original: *Cognition*</note> of architecture. For the <term>Holy of Holies<gloss>the inner sanctuary of the Temple</gloss></term>, which should have been as long as it was wide, was about five ells wide and nearly twenty ells long. He had surrounded the Temple with many high and pointed towers, like Christian churches, and he made the roofs slope together at the top, both of which are not in accordance with antiquity <note>original: *antiquitæt*</note>. The columns Jachin and Boaz, which stood freely, he had built into the wall as pilasters. The interior space of the Temple went up to the vault, and there was no upper chamber, though one existed. The fifteen steps upon which the <term>Songs of Ascents<gloss>original: *Stufen-Psalmen*; Psalms 120–134, traditionally sung by Levites on the fifteen steps of the Temple</gloss></term> were sung were very flat <note>original: *depreß*</note>, almost like circles fitted into one another, totaling not even half a finger high—not to mention several other errors.

X. Notwithstanding these [errors], once the resolution was made

<meta>This page continues the preface, describing the collaboration with a Jewish Rabbi on the physical model of the Temple and its eventual relocation to the Orphanage in Halle. It emphasizes the educational value of a three-dimensional model for understanding Biblical events.</meta>

->Preface.<-

[...he was] determined to likewise create a model of the Temple, and because he <note>Referring to the Jewish Rabbi from Prague mentioned on the previous page.</note> was well-versed in the <term>Talmud<gloss>The central text of Rabbinic Judaism, containing detailed descriptions of the Temple's dimensions and rituals.</gloss></term>, [the author] conferred <note>original Latin: *conferiret*</note> with him several times. He also saw the ground plan, as well as the board upon which it was to be founded <note>original Latin: *fundiret*</note> and the beginning of the building before his departure from here; he testified that it pleased him that his small model had given occasion for an even larger one.

XI. Since that time, work has been carried out on it, and nearly a year was spent until it could be transported <note>original Latin: *transportiret*</note> to the **Orphanage** <note>This refers to the *Waisenhaus* of the Francke Foundations in Halle, a major center of Pietism and education.</note>, where it was destined <note>original Latin: *destiniret*</note> to go.

XII. Efforts are still being made to carry the work further and to add more to it, primarily by including the types <note>original Latin: *Typorum*; symbolic foreshadowings of Christ</note>, sayings <note>original Latin: *Dictorum*</note>, and histories of Holy Scripture <note>original Latin: *Historiarum Scripturæ Sacræ*</note>, so as to make the demonstration <note>original: *Demonstration*</note> of it useful and edifying to the spectator <note>original Latin: *Spectatori*</note>.

XIII. For there one sees the place where the sacrifices were killed, where they were slaughtered, and where they were consumed by fire upon the altar; there one perceives the area where **Zacharias** was struck mute at the Altar of Incense in the Holy Place; where the Pharisee in the Court of the Priests despised the poor tax collector standing at the threshold of the **Gate of Nicanor**; where the Holy Virgin **Mary** presented the child **JESUS** to the **LORD**; where **Judas** threw the silver coins into the Temple. One can, as it were, follow **Christ** at every step, whether He sits in the House of Learning or the **Synagogue** of the Temple in the midst of the teachers, or disputes with the Jews in the **Porch of Solomon**, overturns the tables in the chamber of the money changers, and thereafter drives the buyers and sellers out of the Temple in another area.

XIV. Therefore, for all who are eager to understand the Holy Scripture even in the physical circumstances of the place regarding the Temple, seeing such a model—constructed with all possible diligence and effort—can be nothing but useful. In a single hour of observing it, the mind will form a much more vivid and almost unforgettable idea, profiting more than could be achieved from reading the best author <note>original Latin: *Autoris*</note> over many hours.

XV. In the essential matters <note>original Latin: *In substantialibus*</note>, we will hopefully not err; many of the most prominent and knowledgeable persons in antiquity <note>original: *Antiquität*</note> and architecture <note>original: *Bau-Kunst*</note> from the city and university have inspected the work, and they have not withheld their kind approval <note>original: *Approbation*</note> from this small building.

<meta>This page continues the preface from page 3, describing the interactions between the author and Jewish visitors to the Temple model in Halle. It shifts from the construction of the model to its use as a tool for theological debate and proselytization.</meta>

# ->Preface.<-

XVI. In particular, many Jews, both those from abroad and those residing here in Halle, came with their wives to see the work <note>The "work" refers to the massive physical model of the Temple of Solomon/Herod.</note>. They claimed a certain right to it, as it were, by mostly announcing themselves thus: "Our Temple is supposed to be here," or "We wish to see our Temple." Indeed, some of them even returned a second time to listen to the demonstration <note>original: *demonstration*; refers to the guided lecture or explanation of the model’s Biblical significance.</note>.

XVII. However, they were always given some good guidance and presented with the following: The Messiah is to come from the lineage of David; but neither the lineage of David nor his entire tribe, the Tribe of Judah, is still in existence. Therefore, it has now become impossible for a Messiah to come from the Tribe of Judah, which is no longer present. This argument <note>original: *argument*</note> was formulated from the admission of a Jew who confessed that no more than two tribes of the entire Jewish people still exist, and the Tribe of Judah was not among them.

XVIII. Likewise, not a single person has yet been able to answer the conclusion: Moses destroyed many heathen nations with the sword; but did Moses, or any of all those prophets, convert as many heathen nations from their idolatry to the one God of Heaven and Earth as **Christ**? Therefore, Christ must be greater than Moses and the greatest among all the prophets.

XIX. Furthermore, it was pointed out to them: The Old Testament, which you Jews possess, is indeed the Word of God; and who brought this Old Testament and Word of God into the hands of so many heathens, so that they read it diligently and hold it in high esteem? None other than Christ; therefore He must be a great prophet, indeed the greatest among all the prophets.

XX. The Lord God spoke through the prophet Haggai [chapter] II, verse 10 <note>In many modern Bibles, this is Haggai 2:9.</note>: *The glory of this latter house shall be greater than that of the first*; yet in this latter house or Temple, the well-known five items were missing: 1) The Ark of the Covenant. 2) The Eternal Fire. 3) The Pillar of Cloud. 4) The gift of prophecy, and 5) The Light and Right <term>Light and Right<gloss>A translation of 'Urim and Thummim,' the sacred lot used by the High Priest to determine God's will.</gloss></term>. In what, then, did the glory of this latter house consist, since these five most excellent items were not present? At that point, the prophet pointed with outstretched hand and finger to the Temple, and this one, (which word is found explicitly in the Hebrew <note>original: *expreße im Hebräischen*</note>) which was then being built...

<summary>The author recounts how Jewish visitors claimed a connection to the Temple model and describes the theological arguments used to convince them of Christ's divinity based on the Tribe of Judah and the glory of the Second Temple.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the preface from page 4, which discussed the continuity between the First and Second Temples. It argues that the Second Temple was intended to be a faithful reconstruction of the First, based on the memories of elders who saw both and the divine plans given to David. It then transitions into a critical review of existing scholarly attempts to reconstruct the Temple's architecture.</meta>

...was called the last Temple, so that no other Temple is to be hoped for, because this one would be the last.

XXI. Just as more information is found in the authors <note>original: *Autoribus*</note> concerning this second Temple and its buildings and divisions than regarding the first, it is nevertheless certain that the second Temple was similar to the first in most aspects, including the walls and the buildings. For when the Jews came out of the Babylonian captivity, some were still living who had seen the previous Temple; these were undoubtedly diligently consulted <note>original: *consuliret*</note>, and the foundations of the first walls were sought and found everywhere. They were eager to come as close to the first original <note>original: *original*</note> as was humanly possible, because **God** Himself was the architect of the first Temple as well as the Tabernacle. As King David said when handing over the floor plan of the Temple to Solomon: 

> All this has been given to me in writing by the hand of the **Lord**, so that it instructs me in all the works of the pattern. <note>1 Chronicles 28:19. The "pattern" refers to the divinely inspired architectural design.</note>

XXII. Nothing has been set down here according to personal whim, but rather the guidance of the Holy Scriptures and credible authors <note>original: *Autorum*</note> has been followed. Even so, *Sturmius* in his *Draft of the Jerusalem Temple* <note>original: *Sciagraphia Templi Hierosolymitani*</note>, and *Goldmann’s* otherwise very famous *Architecture* <note>original: *Bau-Kunst*</note>, were of little service here, as they only depicted Ezekiel's Temple <note>original: *templum Ezechielis*; referring to the visionary temple described in the book of Ezekiel, which scholars often distinguish from the historical Temple of Solomon</note>, which there was no intention to build. One also could not build according to *Bünting’s* floor plan and depiction of the Temple, nor could one follow *Adrichomius’s* presentation in his *Theater of the Holy Land* <note>original: *Theatro terræ sanctæ*</note> in all things. *Capellus*, in his *Thrice-Holy* <note>original: *Trisagio*</note> found in *Walton’s Polyglot Bible*, is not clear enough in his threefold description of the Temple and is insufficient <note>original: *insufficient*</note> to represent a complete building from it. We were not able to compare <note>original: *conferiren*</note> *Villalpandus* <note>Juan Bautista Villalpando was a famous 16th-century Jesuit who produced massive, though controversial, reconstructions of the Temple</note>, although it is sufficiently clear from the excerpts <note>original: *Excerptis*</note> that *Capellus* took from him that the judgment <note>original: *Judicium*</note> of that interpreter <note>original: *Interpretis*</note> who translated *Maimonides on the Building of the Temple* <note>original: *Maimonidem de ædificio Templi*</note> is true. In his preface <note>original: *proœmio*</note>, he says that Villalpandus—to whom he otherwise attributes great learning—after 16 years of effort and labor, finally only gave a form to the Jerusalem Temple <note>original: *formam templi Hierosolymitani*</note> which was "not described according to historical truth, but was expressed only as a model of a most splendid and sumptuous building and according to the rules of Vitruvius." <note>original: *quæ non ad historiæ fidem descripta, sed tantum ad splendidissimi & sumptuosissimi ædificii exemplar & ad Vitruvii præceptiones expressa sit*. Vitruvius was a Roman architect; the author is criticizing Villalpandus for making the Temple look too much like a Roman palace rather than a Hebrew sanctuary.</note>

XXIII. In the *English Critical Bibles* <note>original: *Bibliis Criticis Anglicanis*</note>, in *Selden’s On Natural Law according to the Hebrew Discipline* <note>original: *Seldeno de Jure Naturæ secundum disciplinam Hebræorum*</note>, in *Pfeiffer* and his *Vexing Dou-

<meta>This page continues the author's survey of scholarly sources used to design the Temple model. The previous page discussed the scriptural basis and the works of Sturmius and Goldmann. Here, the author evaluates various 17th-century scholars and Jewish authorities like Josephus, the Talmudists, Maimonides, and Rabbi Jacob Judah Leon, whose physical models served as direct inspirations.</meta>

### Preface.

...[foundations], in *Witsius’s Sacred Miscellany on the Mysteries of the Levites' Tabernacle* <note>original: *Witsii Analectis Sacris de Tabernaculi Levitici Mysteriis*</note>, in *Spanheim’s Geographical Description of Jerusalem and the Temple* <note>original: *Spanheimii Descriptione Geographica Hierosolymæ & Templi*</note>, and scattered among other authors <note>original: *Autoribus*</note>, much has been found that is useful for this purpose; likewise, *Dassovius*, in his *Dissertation on Images of Hebrew Matters* <note>original: *Dissertatione de imaginibus rerum Hebraicarum*</note>, has discovered many errors that others have not noticed <note>original: *observiret*</note> until now.

XXIV. In particular, *Josephus*—in his *Jewish Antiquities* <note>original: *Antiquitatibus Judaicis*</note> and *The Jewish War* <note>original: *de bello Judaico*</note>—provides some very good, though not complete or sufficient, information about the Temple. The *Talmudists*, however, in the tractate *On the Measurements of the Temple* <note>original: *de mensuris templi*, likely referring to the Middot</note>, who were closest in time to when the Temple still stood, provide such an account <note>original: *recension*</note> of the buildings that it is highly credible and very useful for such an undertaking <note>original: *proposito*</note>. *L'Empereur*, who translated <note>original: *vertiret*</note> this part of the Jewish Talmud, *On the Measurements of the Temple*, into Latin, added such observations <note>original: *animadversiones*</note> that give the work great clarity <note>original: *Illustration*</note> and make the work easy for anyone wishing to design a model of the Temple.

XXV. Next to these, Rabbi Moses Maimonides' treatise *On the Chosen House* <note>original: *de domo electa*</note>, translated by *Ludovicus de Compiegne de Veil*, well deserved to be consulted. Likewise, Rabbi Jacob Judah Leon’s *Four Books on the Jerusalem Temple* <note>original: *Libr. IV. de templo Hierosolymitano*</note> provided very good information in many parts; for this learned Rabbi, skilled in many languages, also had a model of the Temple in Holland, two cubits wide and three cubits long, which he worked out over several years with great diligence and significant expense. If any author has written thoroughly about the Temple regarding its essential parts <note>original: *partes integrantes*</note>, it is he, because he includes all the smallest and largest details <note>original: *minima und maxima*</note>. *Lightfoot*, in his *Description of the Temple*, is very fundamental <note>original: *fundamental*</note>, and his floor plan has been followed in many respects, yet it was not enough to present <note>original: *sistiren*</note> a complete model.

XXVI. *Lundius*, however, in his *Jewish Sanctuaries* <note>original: *Jüdischen Heiligthümern*</note>, seems to have labored specifically for the sake of our project to determine <note>original: *determiniren*</note> every cubit and half-cubit, so that in the construction of this small Temple model, one could proceed with greater certainty everywhere, and lay out all the walls and areas more successfully. He carries the measuring rod and the plumb line in his hand; we follow in his every footstep, and he, above all others, has been our director of construction <note>original: *Bau-Director*</note>.

XXVII. If we depart from him in a very few instances, he himself—had he been able to know it—would not have disapproved, because visual evidence brings the reason for it into the light of day. For since Lundius the <note>original: *το*, Greek for "the"</note>

<margin>Chel</margin>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's technical critique of Johann Lund’s influential work on Jewish antiquities. Having previously discussed general sources, the author now focuses on specific architectural and ritual discrepancies regarding the Temple's layout and the priestly vestments, justifying the unique design choices made for his own physical model.</meta>

### Preface.

The **Chel** <term>Chel: A Hebrew term for the fortified rampart or space between the outer "Soreg" barrier and the inner Temple walls</term>, or the **intermural space** which lies between the partition wall and the wall of the Temple, is placed by **Lundius** <note>Johann Lund (1638–1686), a German Lutheran pastor and author of a famous work on Jewish antiquities</note> sixteen cubits deep down upon the Temple Mount. However, the Jewish tractate ***On the Measurements of the Temple*** <note>original: *de Mensuris Templi*, referring to the Mishnah tractate *Middot*</note> ***Chapter 1, § 9*** states, on the contrary, that any priest who wished to leave the Temple from the ***subterranean portico*** <note>original: *crypto-porticu*</note> which was beneath the inner court, would have passed under the intermural space toward the gate called **Teri** <note>likely the Tadi Gate</note>. **"He exits through the portico which passes beneath the intermural space and proceeds through the Gate of Teri"** <note>original: *Per porticum, quæ sub intermurali permeat, egressus, pervadit Portam Teri*</note>. Visual evidence also shows that the intermural space must have been elevated and close to the inner court; for the Israelites who wished to offer sacrifices waited for one another in the intermural space, so that as soon as the first group was finished with their sacrifice, others were immediately present to succeed them. This could not have happened so quickly if the intermural space were sixteen cubits deep and every Israelite first had to climb thirty-two steps with their sacrificial animal.

Similarly, in **Lundius's** work, the **Table of Showbread** <note>original: *Schaubrodt-Tische*</note> is shown with a golden crown around the top. Here too, in the figure of the Table of Showbread—which has been represented somewhat larger in our model—we have had to depart from **Lundius**. Although there was a golden crown around the surface of the Table of Showbread pointing downwards, it could not have been around the top edge, because the two bottom-most loaves of showbread lay upon the table itself and, due to their length, projected beyond the table.

**Lundius** had the **Cherubim** fashioned according to the illustration of **Rabbi Leon** <note>Jacob Judah Leon Templo (1602–1675), a Jewish scholar famous for his model of Solomon's Temple</note> in such a way that the two lowest wings are positioned at their loins. However, the Prophet of the Lord, **Ezekiel**, describes them such that the four wings stood side by side, and their hands were covered by these wings. ***Ezekiel 1:8***: **"They had human hands under their wings."** ***Verse 9***: **"And those wings were joined one to another."** <note>original: *Ezech. I, 8. ... vers. 9.*</note> For this reason, we preferred to follow Holy Scripture in this matter and fashion the Cherubim as they are described therein.

In his copperplate engraving, **Lundius** places the **frontal plate** <term>Frontale: The *Tzitz*, a golden plate inscribed with "Holiness to the Lord" worn by the High Priest</term> not so much on the forehead as above the head of the **High Priest**; however, it undoubtedly sat lower. Even **Maimonides** <note>A preeminent medieval Sephardic Jewish philosopher and Torah scholar</note> asserts that the hair of the High Priest's head still emerged above it; therefore, in our model, it has been placed lower on the High Priest.

Regarding the **pinnacle of the Temple** where Satan tempted Christ, **Lundius** is of the opinion that this refers to the outermost wall of the Temple, because a truly extraordinary depth existed there from which Christ was to cast himself down, as a valley lay beside it which was some four hundred cubits deep. But because

<summary>The author justifies deviations from Johann Lund’s Temple model by citing the tractate Middot, the Prophet Ezekiel, and Maimonides regarding the elevation of the Chel, the design of the Showbread table, and the High Priest's attire.</summary>
<keywords>Chel, intermural space, Middot, Johann Lund, Table of Showbread, Cherubim, Ezekiel, Maimonides, High Priest, Temple Pinnacle, Rabbi Jacob Judah Leon</keywords>

### Preface.

...in that same deep valley few people walked, and yet Christ was tempted by Satan through pride at that time, being told he should prove himself as God’s Son before the eyes of all people by casting himself down from the pinnacle of the temple. Therefore, it does not seem likely that by the **pinnacle of the temple** <term>pinnacle: The Greek term *πτερύγιον τοῦ ἱεροῦ* (pterygion tou hierou) refers to the "wing" or highest point of the sanctuary</term> one should understand this outermost wall, which was only six cubits high. Even if we depart from the excellent **Lundius** <note>Johann Lund (1638–1686), whose work *The Old Jewish Sanctuary* was the standard reference of the era</note> in these few points, such a thing does not in the least diminish the due high esteem which one rightly has for the **merits** <note>original Latin: *merita*</note> of the learned and famous **author**, whose tireless diligence God surely now rewards in eternity. Rather, we confess freely and publicly: **Had Lundius not written, we would not have built.**

XXVIII. Initially, two great **difficulties** emerged during the construction. The first: **where authors are lacking** <note>original Latin: *ubi autores deficiunt*</note>; that is, when one was supposed to continue building, yet for the following section, no sufficient information was available in any **author**. The other: **where authors contradict one another** <note>original Latin: *ubi autores sibi invicem contrariantur*</note>; the **authors** describe the intended piece of the construction in different ways, and yet one would like to **reconcile** <note>original: *conciliiren*</note> them, because both were of good **reputation** and were credible. These two **difficulties** were eventually overcome by first confidently building as far as clear information was available; afterward, the necessary **connection** of the buildings finally revealed itself, and one also became visibly aware of which of the **contradicting authors** there was most reason to follow.

XXIX. However, in the presentation of the First Temple, some things from the Second Temple have been added with all diligence **so that its use might be more extensive** <note>original Latin: *ut usus sit amplior*</note>, though this is always noted during the **demonstration** <note>demonstration: the guided tour or explanation of the physical model</note>. For example, the following were not in the First, but in the Second Temple: (1) The two crowns, the gold and the silver ones, which **present themselves** in the uppermost windows of the Temple porch; these were formerly placed upon Joshua the High Priest by the command of God through the prophet Zechariah. (2) The spikes and points of gold that stand on the roof were likewise not in Solomon’s Temple. These were placed there because some among the Jews had formed a completely different **concept** <note>original: *idee*</note> of them. However, because this piece gave occasion for some to almost **protest** against the **model**, claiming it was not the **figure** and representation of the First Temple, but of the Second,

### Preface.

...so one has, also to satisfy those same [authorities], now formed them in such a way that they can be taken away and also put back in place again. (3) The 24 rings in the inner courtyard at the place of slaughter were also not present in the First Temple; however, because the capture of **Jesus Christ** was very clearly represented through them, and the place and space for them was available and remaining, they have also been added. (4) Likewise, the balconies and galleries for the women in the outer court, as well as (5) the two-story hall standing one above the other toward the south in the Court of the Gentiles, were first added during the Second Temple. This building, because it is described very unclearly by most **authors** <note>historical scholars and biblical commentators</note>, was one of the most laborious tasks in this entire small temple-construction.

XXX. The copper-plate engravings which have been added serve the purpose that those who have seen the model of the Temple once can bring all its parts and their locations before their eyes again in such drawings as often as they please; they will also be able to remember the remaining pieces which the engraving cannot present in terms of color and other circumstances. It is also to be expected that such engravings will give those who have not seen the small building, but who are willing to take the trouble to examine these few drawings, some occasion to view many passages of Holy Scripture—where mention of the Temple is made—more clearly and to understand them all the more distinctly.

XXXI. Some pieces have been presented separately in a slightly larger model, which otherwise could not have been distinctly presented in their small proportion relative to the other parts. Such as the **High Priest** in his full vestments, and especially his <term>Breastplate: The *Amt-Schildlein* or *Choshen*, a sacred pectoral worn by the High Priest set with twelve gemstones</term>, in which effort was made to exhibit almost all the gemstones to the **spectator** <note>original: *Spectatori*</note> as oriental stones. Likewise, a <term>Table of Showbread: A golden table used to display twelve loaves of bread representing the tribes of Israel</term> and a golden lampstand with all their belongings are presented in a larger figure, according to the scale of the High Priest.

XXXII. Regarding the present review of the pieces of the Temple, it has for now been kept brief, and in their series and arrangement, an order has been observed which is aimed at the greater benefit of the **spectator**. For so that he may receive a general idea of the whole work right at the beginning, the external buildings are first shown to him all the way around on all four sides, and then the <term>inner sanctum: From the Latin *penetralia*, referring to the most private, interior parts of the temple</term>, the inner courtyard, and the Temple itself, and within it, the Holy and Most Holy Place are opened.

### Preface.

XXXIII. Just as this small building is specifically intended to be demonstrated to all the youth in the schools of the Orphanage <note>The *Waisenhaus* in Halle, a famous educational and charitable institution founded by August Hermann Francke.</note>, so that they may learn to understand much more clearly the Holy Scripture—to which they are daily introduced with all diligence—it has also been found advisable to construct a similar model for the Royal Pedagogium <note>An elite preparatory school for the sons of nobility and gentry</note> in Glaucha <note>A suburb of Halle where the Francke Foundations were located.</note>. Indeed, the construction of this second model has progressed so far that it is nearly completed.

XXXIV. Now, further consideration is being given to creating a model of the city of Jerusalem and the Promised Land, including its most prominent cities, mountains, valleys, rivers, and deserts, as well as the stopping places <note>original: "mansionibus"</note> of the Israelites during their journey from Egypt, so that these may be presented <note>original: "fistiret," from the Latin for 'to place' or 'to stand'</note> before the eyes of Christian viewers <note>original: "Spectatorum"</note>. The floor plans have already been drawn on two large boards prepared for this purpose. We live in the hope that the pleasant variety of so many small buildings, appearing all at once in such great numbers to the eye of the viewer, will be observed with the same benefit <note>original: "Nuß," an archaic spelling of *Nutzen*</note>, edification, and pleasure, if not even greater.

> May the Eternal and All-Good GOD—who has driven away the shadows of the Old Testament through the dawn from on high <note>A reference to Luke 1:78, referring to the coming of Christ.</note>, **Jesus Christ**, and has taken away the veil from the Most Holy Place—reveal the truth of His Divine Word among us more and more. May He graciously grant that we and all Christians become and remain living temples of the Holy Spirit, in whom GOD dwells with His grace, and may He be forever glorified in all His works and benefits! May He allow all still-unconverted Jews and Heathens to reach the knowledge of the truth of **Jesus Christ**, and grant that the entire earth become a temple and sanctuary of His glory and honor. Thus, may the holy and highly praised name of GOD be gloriously exalted by all peoples, languages, and tongues, both here on earth and above in heaven, where GOD Himself will be the Sun and Temple of His chosen ones in the church triumphant, and be glorified from eternity to eternity! Halle, September 13th, in the year <note>original: "Anno"</note> 1717.

# I.

## History of Solomon’s Temple, briefly drafted from the Holy Scripture.

The Almighty and Eternal GOD truly has no need of a temple or a house in which to dwell. He fills heaven and earth with His presence. The largest walls of the most magnificent building cannot enclose the divine essence. All the heavens of heavens may not encompass GOD. And yet, it pleased the all-wise GOD to give the command that a Tabernacle <term>Stifts-Hütte: The portable sanctuary or "Tent of Meeting" used by the Israelites during the Exodus.</term> should be built for Him by Moses as a dwelling, and a Temple in Jerusalem by Solomon as His sanctuary. Both, however, had to be built not according to human discretion, but according to a pattern <note>original: "Fürbilde," meaning a prototype, model, or divine blueprint.</note> which the LORD GOD Himself had given. To Moses, the entire construction of the Tabernacle was shown on Mount Sinai (Exodus 25:9), and King David likewise received the pattern of the Temple in Jerusalem from the hand of the LORD, which he gave to his son, Solomon (1 Chronicles 28:11).

He [David] had indeed intended to build a temple for the LORD himself, and revealed this intention to the prophet Nathan (2 Samuel 7:1–3). But because the LORD GOD would not permit him to do so—since he had led many wars during his reign and still had to contend with many enemies (v. 4, 11; 1 Chronicles 22:8)—he nevertheless made full preparations for the intended Temple construction during his lifetime. He designated the place, namely Mount Moriah, where the Temple was to stand (1 Chronicles 22:1, 17); there, according to the command of GOD through the prophet Gad, he erected an altar at the very spot where later the great Altar of Burnt Offering <term>Brandt-Opfer-Altar: The primary altar for animal sacrifices in the Temple's outer court.</term> was to be placed in the Temple, and when he sacrificed upon it, fire fell from heaven and consumed the burnt offering (v. 18, 30). After this, he gathered all the princes and leaders of the twelve tribes of Israel and publicly commissioned the Temple construction to his son, Solomon, in the presence of them all (1 Chronicles 28:1, 10). He gave him the pattern or the floor plan of all the buildings (v. 11, 12), the order of the priests and Levites (v. 13), and a sketch of the Cherubim <term>Cherubim: Celestial winged beings; in the Temple, large gold-covered figures that stood in the Holy of Holies.</term> (v. 18). He commanded the royal servants, however, that they [should help] his son, So-

to Solomon, for the execution of such a great undertaking, were to faithfully assist him with counsel and deed. (2 Chronicles 3:17–18.) He himself [David] gave toward the Temple construction from his own fortune and the royal treasury many excellent precious stones, three thousand talents <note>original: "Centner," a historical unit of weight; in this biblical context, it refers to the Hebrew talent.</note> of gold from Ophir, and seven thousand talents of silver. (2 Chronicles 30:1, 5.)

The princes of his realm followed the king’s praiseworthy example and gave one thousand talents of gold and ten thousand guilders <note>original: "Gülden," referring here to gold darics or drachmas.</note>, ten thousand talents of silver, eighteen thousand talents of bronze, and one hundred thousand talents of iron; they also presented a great quantity of very costly precious stones for the even more magnificent decoration of the Temple. (verses 6–9.) King David also appointed the builders and stonecutters. (2 Chronicles 3:2.) He procured a great deal of ironwork (verse 3) and had cedar wood brought from Tyre and Sidon (verse 4), so that a very great quantity of all those building materials for the impending Temple construction was at hand. (verse 5.)

When King David eventually fell asleep, old and full of days, and the LORD GOD confirmed the kingdom to Solomon and gave him peace and rest during his fortunate reign (1 Kings 5:4), he earnestly undertook the Temple construction entrusted to him by his father, King David, with GOD’s help. He sent word to Huram <note>Also known as Hiram.</note>, the King of Tyre, and requested that he send a significant number of skilled workmen, which was done. (verses 8, 15.) Thereupon, King Solomon divided the laborers into certain orders. Seventy thousand he appointed to carry burdens, eighty thousand were carpenters <note>original: "Zimmerleute," though in this context it refers generally to stone-quarriers or woodcutters in the mountains.</note>, and three thousand six hundred he appointed as officials, who had to oversee the workers and the entire construction. (2 Chronicles 2:1–2.) Because it was a very difficult labor to establish such an important and extensive building upon a high mountain, the workers were only required to work for one month at a time, and for two months they rested, while others took their place in the work during that interval. (verse 14.)

So that a truly steady and lasting foundation might be laid, very large stones were hewn (verses 17–18), and the workmen immediately prepared the stones and the wood on-site so well and skillfully that during the erection of the building itself on Mount Moriah, not the slightest thing had to be changed; they only had to assemble it. In this regard, it is said that no hammer, nor axe, nor any iron tool was heard during the building. (verse 7.) Now, as everything regarding the construction, through GOD’s grace, went well—

and proceeded happily, so at last this arduous, costly, and very magnificent **Temple construction** was completed within seven years, with GOD, according to every desire. (v. 37. 38.)

Thereupon King *Solomon* assembled all the princes and most prominent members of the twelve tribes in *Jerusalem*, who were all to be present at the solemn **dedication** of the House of the LORD. (1 Kings 8:1, 9.) Everything that King *David* had dedicated in gold, silver, and precious vessels for the divine service was brought up into the Temple, (1 Kings 7:51.) and the priests carried the **Ark of the Covenant** <note>The sacred chest containing the stone tablets of the Ten Commandments.</note> into the **Holy of Holies** <note>The innermost sanctum of the Temple, intended as the dwelling place of God’s presence.</note> and placed it in its designated spot. Also, the **Tabernacle of Moses** <note>The portable tent-sanctuary used by the Israelites during the Exodus.</note>, in which the divine service had been held until now, was carried up into the Temple with all the holy furnishings that were in the Tabernacle, in the presence of the King and a great *procession* <note>original: "Comitat," from the Latin *comitatus*, meaning a retinue or ceremonial escort.</note>. (1 Kings 8:4.)

Now when this had happened, and the golden lampstands, tables of showbread, and the rest were placed in their proper positions in the Temple, and everything there was now fully in order, GOD immediately made His gracious presence known through a visible sign. For a cloud and mist filled the House of the LORD, so that the priests could not stand in the Temple, nor perform their office because of the cloud, for the glory of the LORD filled the House of the LORD. (1 Kings 8:10–11; 2 Chronicles 5:11.) At this, an inward joy and exultation arose among all the people. (1 Kings 8:12–21.) And *Solomon* stepped before the altar of the LORD in the sight of all *Israel*, spread out his hands toward heaven, and in a very beautiful and long prayer, handed over the newly built house to GOD the LORD to be His Temple and Sanctuary. (v. 22–53.)

Immediately afterward, when burnt offerings and other sacrifices were offered and brought upon the high altar, GOD, to testify to His gracious pleasure, caused a very bright fire to fall from heaven in the sight of all the *Israelites* (who were present in an innumerable multitude), which consumed the offerings on the altar with its heat. (2 Chronicles 7:1.) When all the people saw this, they were filled with joy, fell down on their faces to the earth, and lauded and praised GOD. (v. 3–7.) They *continued* <note>original: "continuirten."</note> in such devotion for a full seven days, and when these had been completed with praying, singing, lauding, and thanking amidst much inward joy and testimony, they returned home again, happy and of good cheer over all the good that GOD had done for His people *Israel*. (1 Kings 8:65–66.)

# History

Solomon, for the execution of such a great undertaking, was to be faithfully assisted with counsel and deed. (2 Chron. 3:17, 18.) He himself gave for the building of the **Temple** from his own fortune and the royal treasury many excellent precious stones, three thousand hundredweights <note>original: "Centner," a historical unit of weight roughly equal to 100-110 pounds.</note> of gold from Ophir <note>A legendary port or region mentioned in the Bible, famous for its wealth and high-quality gold.</note>, and seven thousand hundredweights of silver. (1 Chron. 29:3, 4, 5.)

The princes of his realm followed the King’s praiseworthy example and gave one thousand hundredweights of gold and ten thousand guilders <note>original: "Gülden," a gold coin used in various European states.</note>, ten thousand hundredweights of silver, eighteen thousand hundredweights of bronze, and one hundred thousand hundredweights of iron, and presented a great quantity of very costly precious stones for the even more magnificent decoration of the Temple. (v. 6–9.) King David also appointed the builders and stonemasons. (1 Chron. 22:2.) He procured much ironwork (v. 3), and had cedar wood brought from Tyre and Sidon (v. 4), so that a very great quantity of all those building materials was available for the upcoming Temple construction. (v. 5.)

Now, when King David thereafter fell asleep, old and full of days, and GOD the LORD confirmed the kingdom to Solomon and gave him peace and rest during his fortunate reign (1 Kings 5:4), he earnestly undertook the Temple construction entrusted to him by his father, King David, with GOD’s help. He sent to Hiram <note>original: "Huram," the King of Tyre who entered into a famous alliance with Solomon.</note>, the King of Tyre, and requested that he send him a significant number of skilled workmen, which was done. (v. 8–15.) Thereupon King Solomon divided the laborers into certain orders. Seventy thousand he appointed to carry burdens, eighty thousand were carpenters, and three thousand six hundred he appointed as officials <note>original: "Amtleute," meaning overseers or administrators.</note> who were to supervise the workers and the entire construction. (2 Chron. 2:1, 2.) Because it was also a very difficult task to establish such an important and extensive building upon a high mountain, the workers were only permitted to work for one month at a time, and for two months they rested, while others took up the work in the meantime. (v. 14.)

In this regard, it is said that neither hammer, nor axe, nor any iron tool was heard during the building. (v. 7.) Now, while through GOD’s grace everything regarding the construction went well and

# History
<meta>catchword: Incense-</meta>

God Himself, through the prophets of the Israelites, calls this Temple built by Solomon their highest comfort, the delight of their eyes, and the desire of their heart. (Ezekiel 24:25.) This was the only temple in the entire land. All sacrifices were brought here, (2 Chronicles 7:12.) and every male in Israel had to gather there three times each year, where so many thousands of Israelites, with united devotion in their psalms and songs of praise, honored and worshiped the one God of heaven and earth with all the strength of their souls. The Queen of the Kingdom of Arabia <note>Commonly known as the Queen of Sheba.</note> came to Jerusalem not only to hear Solomon's wisdom and to observe the beautiful order of his extensive court, but she had also undertaken the long and arduous journey with the intention of seeing for herself the magnificent buildings erected by the wise king—among which the Temple, though she saw it only from the outside, was the most excellent. Inside, every room was covered with thick gold on the floor, ceiling, and walls; and outside, everything was built from the most beautiful and whitest marble. When those traveling to Jerusalem saw the Temple from afar, it appeared to them like a great, bright, and white mountain of snow; however, when the sun shone upon the roof of the Temple, which was covered with highly polished golden plates, it looked as if many burning torches and fiery rays were breaking forth from this white mountain of snow. But the most excellent thing about the entire Temple was that Almighty God revealed His presence in the pillar of cloud and fire within the Holy of Holies.

Since the Lord God had thus chosen this Temple for His dwelling and had confirmed it through many miracles, it is all the more astonishing that shortly thereafter, this House of God was desecrated by the people of the Jews with so many abominable acts of idolatry. In honor of the idol Baal <note>A major Canaanite deity whose worship often competed with the veneration of the Hebrew God.</note>, a grove or forest (as was the custom among the heathens) was planted in the Temple, and many vessels belonging to it were placed in the House of the Lord. (2 Kings 23:4.) Certain Baalites <note>Priests or followers of Baal.</note> were also appointed to burn incense to Baal and to all the host of heaven <note>Referring to the sun, moon, and stars.</note>. (verse 5.) By the wicked Queen Athalia, the treasure of the House of the Lord was diverted to the idol worship of this cursed Baal. (2 Chronicles 24:7.) Following the manner of the Persians, horses and chariots were kept in the House of the Lord in honor of the sun. (2 Kings 23:11.) Ahaz, the wicked King of Judah, had all the doors in the House of the Lord closed. Public worship could no longer be held. (2 Chronicles 28:24.) The golden lampstands were extinguished. No incense-

<summary>This page describes the architectural splendor and divine significance of Solomon's Temple before detailing its later desecration by various kings and queens who introduced pagan idol worship into the sanctuary.</summary>
<keywords>Solomon's Temple, Jerusalem, Queen of Sheba, architecture, marble and gold, idolatry, Baal, Queen Athalia, King Ahaz, biblical history</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Solomon's Temple, transitioning from the previous page's description of its golden splendor to its spiritual and physical decline under later kings.</meta>

<page-num>15</page-num>
<header>of Solomon's Temple.</header>

Incense was no longer burned upon the Altar of Incense, and no more burnt offerings were brought upon the Altar of Burnt Offerings. (2 Chronicles 29:7, 8.) The King in *Judah*, *Manasseh*, who was a sorcerer and a murderer, had pagan altars built in both courts of the House of the Lord. He even had pagan idols placed within the Temple and led the people by his example in how they should serve such idols (2 Chronicles 29:7, 8, 9) so that they might also obtain the good fortune of those nations who worshiped them—which was usually the goal of such idolatry. Following the ways and customs of the Egyptians, all sorts of images—both of creeping things and of beasts—were set up in the chambers of the House of the Lord (*Ezekiel* 8:10). Thus, the Temple, which God had chosen for His dwelling and sanctuary, was very often desecrated and dishonored in a terrifying manner by the blinded Jewish people through shameful idolatry.

Since many other abominations were also in vogue alongside this—as the haughty people, floating in pure bliss, did everything their sinful hearts craved and their eyes desired—God finally began to intervene. He awoke to punish them and threw the glory of *Israel* from heaven down to earth. (*Lamentations* 2:1 <note>original: "Thren." refers to *Threni*, the Latin title for the Lamentations of Jeremiah.</note>.) He made the roads to *Zion* desolate (ch. 1:4) and filled all the inhabitants with misery on the day of His fierce anger (ch. 1:12). The Lord caused both the festivals and the Sabbath to be forgotten (ch. 2:6), rejected His altar, and abandoned His sanctuary (v. 7). He kindled a fire which, through its heat, also consumed their very foundations. For the righteous God led the *Chaldeans* <note>The Babylonians, led by King Nebuchadnezzar II.</note> (ch. 4:11) against them—a cruel and fierce people who came into the Jewish land with a hostile army and devastated everything with fire and sword. God gave their weapons a complete victory everywhere. No one had the courage to fight against them. Everyone fled before them, yet very few could escape their cruelty. Before them, the land was like a paradise; after them, like a desolate wilderness. But *Jerusalem* and its conquest was the main objective of their campaign. They surrounded it with a great military force (2 Chronicles 25:1) <note>The source citation refers to the siege narrative; traditionally this is found in 2 Kings 25 or 2 Chronicles 36.</note>; for two whole years they besieged and distressed it. When they finally broke into the city by force, they committed terrible acts of hostility. Everything they encountered was cut down. King *Zedekiah* was captured, bound with chains, and—after seeing his children pitifully slaughtered, he was robbed of both his eyes.

<header>Model</header>

(2 Chronicles 25:5.) Some of the inhabitants fled into the Temple to find safety there from the bloodthirsty sword of the enraged enemy. But even in the House of the Sanctuary, no person was spared. (2 Chronicles 36:17.) The priests were strangled, the altar desecrated, the treasure of the House of the Lord plundered, the bronze sea and the large bronze pillars at the House of the Lord broken into pieces. (2 Kings 25:13, 17.) The walls were torn down and leveled to the ground, and everything else burned with fire, so that through the Chaldeans the magnificent building of the Temple of *Solomon*, famous throughout the entire *Orient*, after it had stood not much longer than four hundred years, was destroyed from its foundations and completely laid in ashes.

## II.
## Model of Solomon's Temple and Presentation 
## of all those pieces / which were to 
## be seen upon the same.

1) Mount Moriah <note>original Hebrew: הַר הַמּוֹרִיָה (*Har ha-Moriah*)</note>, which was 500 cubits long and wide, upon which the Temple was built. (2 Chronicles 3:1.) On this mountain, *Abraham* was to slaughter and sacrifice his son. (Genesis 22:2.) Here also, during an outbreak of pestilence, *David* saw the angel of the Lord and, at God’s command, built an altar. (2 Samuel 24:16, 18.) This place where the altar and Temple were built belonged, however, to *Arnan* <note>In English biblical translations, this figure is usually called Araunah or Ornan.</note>, the Jebusite, a pagan <note>German: *Heyden*. The author highlights that the Temple site was purchased from a non-Jew (a Gentile) to symbolize the future inclusion of all nations in the Church.</note>, from whom King *David* purchased the site. By this, the calling of the pagans to the Church of God in the New Testament was already clearly indicated at that time. In *Abraham's* time, this mountain was desolate, uneven, and full of thorns and hedges. For when *Abraham* had already bound his son *Isaac* and intended to slaughter and sacrifice him, God called out to him: "Do not lay your hand on the boy, and do nothing to him, for now I know that you fear God and have not spared your only son for my sake"; then *Abraham* lifted up his eyes and saw a ram behind him hanging by its horns in the thicket. (Genesis 22:12, 13.) *Solomon*, however, had this uneven and thorn-filled mountain leveled into many beautiful plains through the application of great labor, so that one could [go] step-by-step toward the Sanctuary of the Lord

...approached ever closer, so that the Temple itself stood upon the highest point of the mountain.

2) The outermost wall, which surrounded the site on the outside so that no one walking there would be in danger of falling, because the adjacent valley on the eastern and southern sides was 400 cubits deep <note>Approximately 600 feet or 180 meters.</note>.

3) The Gate toward the East in this outermost wall, from which a bridge led across the valley to the Mount of Olives; it was there that the disciples showed the buildings of the Temple to Christ, but he answered them: "Do you not see all these things? Truly, I say to you, there shall not be left here one stone upon another that shall not be thrown down." Matthew 24:2. It was also there that Christ’s final suffering began <note>A reference to the Garden of Gethsemane at the foot of the Mount of Olives.</note>. In that same place, his Ascension also occurred.

4) The Plain, an area of considerable space located between the outermost 6-cubit-high wall and the porticos of the Court of the Gentiles.

5) The Temple Mount <term>Har ha-Bayit <note>original Hebrew: הַר הַבַּיִת</note></term>, which is also called the Court of the Gentiles <term>Chatzar ha-Leumim <note>original Hebrew: חֲצַר הַלְּאוּמִּים</note></term>, because Gentiles were permitted to come as far as this point. 1 Kings 8:41, 42, 43. However, the Gentiles were regarded in three different ways. Some were absolute strangers—Gentiles who still worshipped their idols. Some were called "Strangers in the Gate," who were permitted to live among the Jews everywhere except in the city of Jerusalem. These accepted the Seven Laws of the Children of Noah <note>The Noachide Laws: a set of moral imperatives that, according to the Talmud, were given by God as a binding set of laws for all of humanity.</note>, namely that they guarded themselves against idolatry, blasphemy of God’s name, murder, unchastity, theft, injustice, and eating blood. Finally, some of the Gentiles were called "Strangers of Righteousness," or "Associates of the Jews," who had completely abandoned the pagan religion and embraced Judaism. These last ones, the Jewish associates, were permitted to go into the outer and inner courts of the Temple as far as the Court of Israel. The first two types of Gentiles, however, were not permitted to go further than the Court of the Gentiles, up to the dividing wall. This Court of the Gentiles was much larger than the outer court and the Court of Israel; by this, God indicated that in the future, the number of Gentiles who would worship the true God in the Church of the New Testament would be far greater than the number of Jews then living within the narrow borders of the Promised Land.

6) The Porticos or walkways, which surrounded the Court of the Gentiles on all four sides.

7) Solomon’s Portico <term>Ulam Shelomoh <note>original Hebrew: אוּלָם שְׁלֹמֹה</note></term>, located toward the east, and so...

On such roofs, the idolatrous Jews performed their idol worship. However, so that an Israelite who had touched a dead body, or human bones, or even a grave, and had become unclean, might be purified again, the Lord God had ordained that a red heifer, which was "without blemish"—that is, without fault or defect—must be burned. The priest who was to perform this burning remained in the Temple for seven full days beforehand. Thereupon, the heifer was led out of the Temple toward the Mount of Olives, bound and placed upon the woodpile, slaughtered, and its blood sprinkled toward the Temple. When the fire was lit, cedar wood, scarlet, and hyssop were thrown into it. The ashes of the red heifer were carefully preserved. Now, when the Israelite was to be purified, a little of this ash was taken and water was poured over it, which was drawn in stone cups either from the spring of Siloam or Gihon (the locations of which can be seen in the model of the city of Jerusalem). The sprinkling, however, was performed with a bunch of hyssop, which was dipped into this water mixed with ashes. The penitent David refers to this ceremony of the Levitical law in the words of the 51st Psalm, verse 7: **Purge me with hyssop, and I shall be clean; wash me, and I shall be whiter than snow.** And such a purification had a specific aim toward the atonement which [was achieved] through Jesus

14) The guard-chamber of the Levites, which were located by all the gates. There were twenty-one such Levite watches in the Temple. This was done not so much out of concern that someone might rob the treasury of the House of the LORD—for they were of the opinion that as long as God was gracious to the people, He would protect His Temple Himself—but rather, these many watches were kept so that they might honor God’s power, glory, and majesty through such attendance. However, these watches also simultaneously signified spiritual vigilance, as a faithful person should always be alert, watchful, and ready in faith for the coming of the Lord Jesus Christ, who reminds His faithful of this in Matthew 24:42. **Watch therefore, for you do not know what hour your Lord will come.** And in chapter 26:41: **Watch and pray, that you enter not into temptation.**

15) The room above this chamber, in which the Great Sanhedrin <term>Synedrium <note>original Greek/Latin: the supreme council and court of justice for the Jewish people</note></term> met after it had departed from the <term>Chamber of Hewn Stone <note>original Latin: *Conclavi Gazith*; the traditional meeting place of the Sanhedrin on the south side of the Temple's inner court</note></term>, as happened several times. In the Scriptures, this room is called the Princes' Chamber, which was located above the guard-chamber. For as sins and misdeeds increased among the Jewish people—especially during the time of the Second Temple—to the point that some had earned the death penalty for such abominable acts, the High Priests and elders of the people did not wish to hand over a sinning Israelite to the heathens <note>meaning the Roman authorities</note> because they no longer had the power to put anyone to death themselves. They feared that the heathens, due to their hatred and contempt for the Jews, would treat them too unmercifully. In order to keep the sinful, godless Israelites alive, they met together...

From this, the Prophet took the opportunity to rebuke the Jews and hold them to account: These people obey the voice and commandment of a mortal man, yet you will not obey the word and command of Almighty God. Jeremiah 35:4 and the following verses.

17) The Beautiful Gate of the Temple, which was directly opposite the Susa Gate <note>The eastern gate of the Temple complex, discussed on the previous page</note>. it led into the outer court, or the Court of the Women; it was also covered in gold and more adorned <note>original: *exorniret*</note> than the others.

18) The actual location before this Beautiful Gate of the Temple where Peter and John healed the man who had been lame from his mother’s womb. Acts 3:2. For as they both wished to go into the Temple at the ninth hour—which was approximately three o’clock in the afternoon, because the Jews divided every day in both winter and summer into twelve equal parts—they found this lame man. He allowed himself to be carried and placed there daily before the Temple gate, which is called the Beautiful, so that he might beg for alms from those entering the Temple. Now, when he saw Peter and John about to enter the Temple, he asked for alms. But Peter looked at him, together with John, and said: "Look at us." And he looked at them, expecting to receive something from them. But Peter said: "Silver and gold have I none; but what I have, that I give to you. In the name of JESUS CHRIST of Nazareth, rise up and walk!" And he took him by the right hand and lifted him up. Immediately his legs and ankles became firm; he leaped up, could walk and stand, and went with them into the Temple, walking and leaping

This buying and selling of sacrificial animals eventually moved closer to the Temple, so that such a marketplace was established on the **Mount of Olives** opposite the Temple. Finally, however, the sacrificial animals were even brought into the Temple itself for sale, so that an open market was held, as it were, in the **Court of the Gentiles**. Because everyone who wished to go into the Temple always had to pass by there, the **divine service** was disturbed by the shouting and lowing of the livestock as well as the commotion of the many people; furthermore, the **House of GOD** was dishonored by the greed and usury practiced there. Therefore, the **Lord JESUS** displayed a holy zeal against it and a flash of His majesty; He went into the Temple and forcibly drove out those who bought and sold, and said: It is written, My house is a **house of prayer**, but you have made it into a **den of thieves** <note>original: *Mörder-Gruben*, literally "murderers' den"</note>. **Luke 19:45–46.**

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Court of the Gentiles in the Temple model, explaining the spiritual and legal reasons for the separation between Israelites and non-Israelites, and how this separation was eventually resolved in Christian theology.</meta>

# Model

...come / but no further. In this, the Israelites were to be reminded that they should separate themselves from all pagan, godless ways. For the Gentiles <note>The term "Gentiles" or "pagans" (Heyden) refers to non-Jewish nations.</note> were outside the citizenship of Israel / and strangers to the testaments of the promise / therefore they had no hope, Ephesians 2:12. and were without God in the world / whose understanding is darkened / and they are alienated from the life / that is from God through the ignorance / that is in them / through the blindness of their heart / who are reckless / and give themselves over to indecency / and practice all kinds of impurity along with greed. Ephesians 4:18-19. Therefore, God wanted them to be separated from pagan ways. Jeremiah 10:2: Thus says the Lord / you shall not learn the way of the Gentiles.

God had indeed already distinguished the Israelites from other peoples and Gentiles through the ordinance and introduction of the Sabbath day, through the solemn revelation and proclamation <note>original: "Promulgation"</note> of His Law on Mount Sinai / through the sending of His holy prophets and the preaching of His Word. Psalm 147:20: He does not do so for any Gentile / nor does He let them know His laws.

The Lord God also commanded the Israelites in His Law such things as were entirely contrary to the customs and laws of the Gentiles. Their altar was to be without steps. No honey was allowed to come to even a single one of their sacrifices. They were not permitted to carve any mark or sign into their skin, nor touch any dead body, nor sow two kinds of seed in one field, nor even completely cut down the grain during the harvest, but were to leave something in the field for the poor widows and orphans, so that they might be led away from pagan idolatry, falsehood, and unmercifulness, and be kept very far removed from pagan ways and sins.

But because in the New Testament many Gentiles later accepted the word of the Gospel <note>original: "Evangelii"</note>, and turned from their pagan abominations to God, it was shown to them that they should become partakers of all the benefits of God in Christ Jesus, so that there should no longer be a difference between a believing Gentile and a believing Israelite, but that the dividing wall <note>The "dividing wall" or *Soreg* was a physical barrier in the Temple complex that separated the Court of the Gentiles from the inner sanctified areas; passing it was a capital offense for non-Jews.</note> should be taken away, and there should be one shepherd and one flock. Of this, the holy Apostle Paul assures the Ephesians, who formerly had been Gentiles, but had now accepted the Christian faith, as he writes to them: But now / you who are in Christ / and once were far off / have now become near through the blood of Christ. For He is our peace / who has made of both one...

### of Solomon's Temple.

has made / and has broken down the **middle wall of the partition** <note>original Greek: *τὸ μεσότοιχον τοῦ φραγμοῦ*</note>, the fence / that was between them. Ephesians 2:14.

34) The low stonework of the same, made of marble / one cubit <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length, roughly the distance from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger.</note> high, and the latticework of wood two cubits high.

35) The columns, which stood in such latticework at a convenient distance from one another.

36) The entrances through the partition wall.

37) The inscription upon them, where it was written in Greek and Latin letters, that no Gentile should approach further under penalty of death.

38) The thirteen breaches in the partition wall <note>Hebrew: *Soreg* סורג, the latticed barrier</note>, which were made by the Syrians and were later restored by the Jews. For when the enemies of the people of Israel gained power in the land and invaded the city of Jerusalem with force, no one could prevent them from entering the Temple as well. When they saw the inscription above the columns in the partition wall, in which the Gentiles were forbidden further entry into the Temple, they were seized with resentment and grim anger against it, and they tore down this partition wall with force. This happened in thirteen places. But when peace came afterward, and the Israelites regained their rest, they repaired the partition wall in those places; however, the breaches that had been made could still be clearly recognized. Now, when an Israelite came to such a place where such a <term>breach <note>original: "ruptur"</note></term> had occurred, he offered a prayer in which he thanked GOD that He had restrained the power of the enemies at that time, and called upon Him to continue being the almighty protector of the Temple and the people of Israel.

39) The space between the partition wall and the main wall / <note>Hebrew: *Chayil* חיל; Latin: *spatium inter murale*, meaning the space between the walls</note>.

40) The money-changers' chamber, where the money-changers <note>Hebrew: *Shulchanim* שולחנים</note> sat at 13 tables and collected the half-shekel, which every Israelite from 20 years of age and older had to give annually, whether he was rich or poor. Although such a half-shekel only amounted to about 6 <term>groschen <note>A common silver coin in the translator's time, used here to help the reader understand the value</note></term> from each person, it nonetheless added up to a great sum annually because of the multitude of those who gave it, for the Jewish land was very populous. However, this half-shekel had to be paid in its established and customary coin-type, which those who did not have it were obliged to exchange for that reason. ...usury <note>original: *mäßiger Wucher*, literally "moderate usury," though likely implying the systematic practice of money-lending</note> was practiced, so Christ entered this money-changers’ chamber, overturned the money-changers’ tables, and said to them: It is written: My house shall be a house of prayer / but you have made it a den of thieves. *Matthew* 21:12–13; *Luke* 19:45–46.

41) The House of Learning or **Synagogue** in the Temple <note>Hebrew: *Beit HaMidrash* בֵּית הַמִדְרָשׁ, a place dedicated to Torah study and discussion</note>, where the Law was explained on the Sabbath and high festivals. It opened out into the **intermurale** <note>the space between the walls</note> on the south side, leading into the Court of the Gentiles near the inner court. When the sacrificial service on the Sabbath and festival days was completed, people would proceed to the **Synagogue** or House of Learning. It was the priests and Levites who held the office of teaching therein. Everyone had the freedom to ask whatever they wished, and the high **Sanhedrin** <note>original: *Synedrium*, the supreme council and court of ancient Israel</note> was then present, which held no court sessions on the Sabbath or festival days. The teachers were at an elevated, higher place and the listeners at a lower one. The Holy Apostle Paul refers to this custom in the **synagogues** when he answered the Jews who rebelled against him in Jerusalem and accused him of desecrating their Temple, saying: I am a Jewish man / born in Tarsus in **Cilicia** / and brought up in this city at the feet of **Gamaliel** / and taught according to the perfect manner of the law of the fathers. *Acts* 22:3. In this **Synagogue** or House of Learning in the Temple, the child JESUS was found, when he was twelve years old, sitting in the midst of the teachers. This was something quite unusual. For such youths, and all students and listeners, were required to stand at the feet of the teachers. However, the child JESUS, because of his understanding in questions and answers, was extraordinarily called up and taken to the elevated place and **Cathedra** <note>original: *Catheder*, a formal teacher’s chair or throne</note>, where the teachers, out of high regard for him, placed him beside themselves. For they found him in the Temple sitting in the midst of the teachers. *Luke* 2:46. His mother, the Holy Virgin Mary, was dismayed by this and lamented that she had sought Him for three days with sorrow. But He answered her: I must be about / that which is my Father’s. v. 49.

42) The Supreme Court Chamber <note>Hebrew: *Lishkat ha-Gazit* לִשְׁכַּת הַגָּזִית, the Chamber of Hewn Stone</note>, where the high **Sanhedrin**, consisting of 71 persons, met daily and remained together from the morning sacrifice until the evening sacrifice. Seventy-one persons belonged to this **Sanhedrin**, and it had its beginning and origin from Moses, who, according to the advice of **Jethro** his

### of Solomon’s Temple.

his father-in-law <note>The text refers to Jethro, who advised Moses to appoint judges over the people of Israel.</note>, chose men who feared **God**, who were truthful, and who were enemies of greed (*Exod.* 18, 22) so that they might hold court. This **Sanhedrin** <note>original: *Synedrium*, the supreme legal council and high court of ancient Israel</note> was the highest court in the land, from which one could not appeal any higher. The **President** <note>original: *Præses*</note> of the same, who was presumably the High Priest, sat facing West, and the other **Associate Judges** <note>original: *Assessores*</note> sat in a half-circle around him. Below them on the right sat a scribe who recorded the votes of those who acquitted the accused; on the left sat a scribe who recorded the votes of those who **condemned** <note>original: *condemnireten*</note> the accused. **Christ** points toward this custom when he recounts the proceedings of the Last Judgment, saying that he will place the faithful and the righteous on his right hand, and the unbelieving and the godless on his left. *Matth.* 25, 33. In the New Testament, this high **Sanhedrin** is understood to be the High Priests and the Elders of the people who took counsel against **Christ**. **Christ** was in this supreme court chamber when he was falsely accused. *Luke* 22, 66. And as soon as it was day, the elders of the people, the high priests, and the scribes gathered together and led him **into their council** <note>original Greek: *εἰς τὸ συνέδριον ἑαυτῶν*</note>. When Judas regretted that he had betrayed the innocent **Jesus**, he went into the Temple where the **Sanhedrin** was still assembled in this supreme court chamber, for they remained there from the morning sacrifice until the evening sacrifice. *Matth.* 27, 3–4. Then Judas, who had betrayed Him, when he saw that he was condemned to death, repented, and brought again the thirty pieces of silver to the high priests and the elders, and said: I have done evil in that I have betrayed innocent blood. However, in the great anxieties of his evil conscience, he found little comfort from them, for they said to him: What is that to us? See you to that. And because they would not take the thirty pieces of silver back from him, he threw them down at their feet in this supreme court chamber. *verse* 5. And he cast down the pieces of silver in the Temple, departed, went away in total despair, and hanged himself. When the holy Stephen, full of faith and power, did many wonders and great signs among the people, certain men rose up against him and said: We have heard him speak blasphemous words against Moses and against **God**; and they stirred up the people, and the elders, and the scribes, and came upon him, and caught him, and brought him **into the council** <note>original Greek: *εἰς τὸ συνέδριον*</note>. *Acts* 6, 8, 11, 12. The Apostle Paul stood here as well. For when he was accused in Jerusalem of having led Greeks into the Temple—an act that caused a great riot—the Roman Captain stationed at the Fortress Antonia had him taken prisoner. The following day, he brought Paul before the **Sanhedrin** <note>original: *Synedrio*; the supreme council of the Jewish people</note>. *Acts* 22, 30.

### 43) The other part of this upper judicial chamber faces toward the inner courtyard. It was here that lots were cast early in the morning to determine which specific duties each priest would perform during that day's divine service.

### 44) The Wood Chamber <note>original Latin: *Camera ligni*; Hebrew: לשכת העץ, *Lishkat ha-Etz*</note>, where all the wood was carried past when it was brought through the Fire Gate into the inner courtyard. It was also called the Chamber of the Counselors <note>original Hebrew: לשכת פלהדרין, *Lishkat Palhedrin*; Latin: *camera assessorum*</note>, because in this room the High Priest often deliberated with the other priests on matters concerning divine service. He also resided here whenever he was present at the Temple. In this chamber, he was required to stay continuously for seven days prior to the Day of Atonement <note>original: *Versöhnungs-Fest*</note>. The High Priest's garments were also kept safely here.

### 45) The Wheel Chamber / Well or Draw-well Chamber <note>original Hebrew: לשכת הגולה, *Lishkat ha-Golah*</note>, in which there was a well. Water was drawn up from it using a wheel, which the priests drank during their time of service in the Temple. This was because they had to abstain from all wine so that they could maintain a clear distinction between the holy and the unholy. *Levit.* 10, 9.

### 46) The Fire Gate <note>original Hebrew: שער הדלק, *Sha'ar ha-Delek*</note>, through which all the wood intended for the Altar of Burnt Offering was carried.

### 47) The Firstborn Gate <note>original Hebrew: שער הבכורות, *Sha'ar ha-Bekhorot*</note> through which all firstborn sacrificial animals were brought into the inner courtyard and slaughtered on the southern side. *Deuteron.* 15, 19. No firstborn male animal could be redeemed with money or replaced by another sacrificial animal. Furthermore, the head of the household was not permitted to use them for labor or to slaughter and eat them for himself; rather, they had to be brought to the Temple in Jerusalem, no matter how long or distant the journey. However, the animal was kept at home for seven days to be nursed by its mother before being brought to the Temple. For no firstborn sacrificial animal could be slaughtered before the eighth day; *Exod.* 22, 30. This was because the suffering of **Christ**, whom all these sacrifices foreshadowed, was to begin on the eighth day with his circumcision. The "holy" sacrifices were slaughtered on the southern side, while the "most holy" sacrifices were slaughtered on the northern side at the slaughtering pillars and the slaughtering tables located there.

<meta>catchword: 48) The</meta>
