<page-num>29</page-num>
<header>of the Temple of Solomon.</header>

### 48) The Water Gate <note>Hebrew: שער המים, *Sha’ar HaMayim*</note>

Beneath this gate was the Ethan Spring, which had its source in the Garden of Solomon and provided water to the entire Temple. Through this gate, the water from the Pool of Siloam was brought during the **Feast of Tabernacles** <note>original: *Lauber-Hütten-Fest*; also known as Sukkot</note>. This water was offered to God while the people sang: *With joy you will draw water from the wells of salvation.* Isaiah 12:3 <note>The original text cites Isaiah 22:3, likely a printing error for 12:3</note>.

### 49) The Chamber of Avtinas <note>Hebrew: בית אבטינס, *Beit Avtinas*</note>

This was the chamber where the incense was prepared, which the priests lit every morning and evening on the Altar of Incense in the Holy Place. *Exodus* 30:9, 34. This chamber was named after a skillful man who knew how to prepare the incense so well that it would always rise from the glowing coals in a straight pillar like a column. There were four spices commanded by God to be used in the incense: **Balsam**, **Stacte**, **Galbanum**, and **pure Frankincense**. *Exodus* 30:34. 

However, the Jews recount thirteen species that were used in the mixture. These were pounded in golden mortars until they became a fine dust. No person was permitted to replicate this mixture for use in their own home. Half a pound was used for the morning and evening offerings respectively, so that one pound was used daily, totaling 365 pounds burned each year. Because this incense was among the most holy things of the Temple, the priests themselves had to prepare it.

### 50) The Room next to the Chamber of Avtinas

This room contained a priestly guard post, facing out toward the **Court of the Gentiles** <note>original: *Vorhof der Heyden*; the outermost area of the Temple complex accessible to non-Jews</note>.

### 51) The Grove or small forest of trees <note>Hebrew: אשירה, *Asherah*</note>

This was located in the courtyard of the House of the Lord, where **Manasseh**, the wicked King of Judah, practiced idolatry. For he built altars to all the host of heaven in both courtyards of the House of the Lord. *2 Kings* 21:4. Also Manasseh's son, **Amon**, King of Judah, served the idols his father had served and worshiped them. *2 Kings* 21:21. But **Josiah**, the righteous King of Judah, had the grove cut down, carried out of the House of the Lord and the city of Jerusalem, and burned with fire at the Brook Kidron. *2 Kings* 23:6.

### 52) The South Side

This area on the noon-side <note>the South</note> of the inner courtyard was a place where no upper chambers for living quarters were permitted. No mortal man was to live there, because directly opposite, in the **Holy of Holies** <note>original: *Allerheiligsten*</note>, the Almighty God dwelt in the pillar of cloud and fire.

53) The wall toward the **West** <note>original: *gegen Abend*, literally "toward the evening"</note> in the inner courtyard, which for the same reason had no chambers or dwellings, for it was a continuous wall from **South** <note>original: *Mittag*, literally "midday"</note> to **North** <note>original: *Mitternacht*, literally "midnight"</note>.

54) The outermost low wall, six cubits high, toward the **West**.

55) The four gates in this wall, where one descended by steps toward one part of the city; these steps and stairs can be seen on the model of the city of Jerusalem.

56) The portico toward the **West**, and the **Gate of Kipponos** <note>Hebrew: שַׁעַר קִיפּוֹנוֹס, *Sha’ar Kipponos*</note> within it, which was named after the person who built it.

57) The chamber behind the **Most Holy Place**, which takes its name from the curtains.

58) The Levite guard post located there.

59) The portico toward the **North**, which had three rows of columns and was 30 cubits wide.

60) The **Gate of Teri** <note>original: שַׁעַר טֶרִי, *Sha’ar Teri*; likely referring to the Tadi Gate</note> in this portico, through which there was little entry into the Temple. For not far from it lay the **Fortress Antonia**, in which Roman soldiers were stationed as a garrison; they often mocked the Jews who went to the Temple. Anyone who had become <term>unclean</term> <note>ritually impure according to Jewish Law</note> left the Temple through this gate.

61) The outermost wall, six cubits high, behind the porticos.

62) The three gates in this wall.

63) The gate toward the **East** <note>original: *gegen Morgen*, literally "toward the morning"</note>, through which the sacrificial animals were brought into the Temple, after they had first been washed in the pool <note>original: *See*, meaning a lake or large basin</note> lying not far from there.

64) The second gate in the middle of the **Northern** side, which led down to the street in the city called the **Merchant’s Street** <note>Latin: *platea negotiationum*</note>.

65) The third gate toward the **West**, which opened toward the **Fortress Antonia**. This fortress was a strong, well-fortified castle built on a high and steep rock, from which one could look over all the courtyards of the Temple, as its situation is clearly recognizable from the model of the city of Jerusalem. The Fortress Antonia sat upon a high rock. It was formerly called **Baris**, and the very precious garments of the **High Priest** were kept there; these were always fetched from there whenever the High Priest had to appear in the Temple in his full <term>ornamentation</term> <note>ceremonial vestments</note>. King **Herod** turned this Tower of Baris into a very magnificent building.

# of Solomon’s Temple.
<meta>catchword: a</meta>

At the four corners of the fortress stood four towers; three of these were fifty cubits high, but the fourth, which stood toward the **East** <note>original: *Morgen*, "morning"</note> and **South** <note>original: *Mittag*, "midday"</note>, was seventy cubits high. The fortress had a large circumference and contained so many buildings that it resembled a small city. It was separated from Mount Moriah, upon which the Temple sat, by a deep valley, and likewise from the mountain or hill of Bezetha, which was situated to the **North** <note>original: *Mitternacht*, "midnight"</note> of the Fortress Antonia. However, one could still walk from the fortress across a bridge into the Temple; this occurred especially during high festivals, when the Roman Captains and their soldiers had to be present so that no tumult or riot would arise among the very great multitude of people. The Captain who was stationed in this Fortress Antonia is also called the "Captain of the Temple" in Acts 4:1, who took Peter and John prisoner after they had healed the lame man. Whenever a Captain and his soldiers are mentioned together, this Captain is the one to be understood, whereas otherwise those were also called "Captains" who were placed in charge of the most distinguished matters and important duties within the Temple. Herod named this Fortress Antonia after his good friend, Mark Antony <note>original: *Marco Antonio*</note>.

66) The area on the **Northern** side in the inner courtyard, where likewise there were no upper chambers on the inside; thus, no one was permitted to live there, because **GOD** dwelt directly opposite in the Most Holy Place.

67) The kitchen of the priests, where that which was given to them to eat from the sacrifices was cooked. <term>House of the Cooks <note>Hebrew: בֵּית הַמְּבַשְׁלִים, *Beth ha-Mevashlim*</note></term>

68) The Hearth Gate <term>Gate of the Fire <note>Hebrew: שַׁעַר הַמּוֹקֵד, *Sha’ar ha-Moked*</note></term>, in which a priestly guard and a constant fire were maintained so that the priests, who walked barefoot on the marble pavement and often caught cold, could warm themselves again. The priests had to endure much hardship during their sacrificial service in the Temple, because they had to go barefoot, wear very thin linen clothes, drink nothing but water, keep many vigils, and eat unleavened bread for as long as their service in the Temple lasted. This was a prototype for the spiritual priests of the New Testament—which all Christians are meant to be—reminding them that they should not imagine their divine service and Christianity to be purely a matter of happiness, but should know that "we must through much tribulation enter into the kingdom of **God**." Acts 14:22.

...a marble stone lay there, one cubit long and wide, which had a ring on top so that it could be lifted off. Into this hollow, all the keys of the Temple were brought every evening and hung upon a golden chain. No one was permitted to unlock or lock any doors in the Temple except for that priest to whom this duty was assigned. Upon this, the words of Revelation 3:7 are focused: "These things saith he that is holy, he that is true, he that hath the key of David, he that openeth, and no man shutteth; and shutteth, and no man openeth." <note>This verse describes the absolute authority of Christ using the imagery of the Temple's chief key-holder.</note> When these keys of the Temple had been brought to their place by the priest, he covered the hollow with the marble stone, laid himself down upon it, and took his rest there, so that everything might be better guarded.

70) The exit in the inner courtyard from this Fire Gate led through a portal that went out into the same court. Through this portal, the Captain of the Temple went very early with his men to inspect everywhere and see if everything was ready for the divine service of that day. If he encountered a Levite sleeping, he had the power to strike him and to set his clothes on fire with the burning torches that he always had carried before him. When this happened, such Levites often threw off their burning clothes in great terror and fled away naked and bare. Christ’s words are focused on this when he says in Matthew 24:42: "Watch therefore: for ye know not what hour your <term>Lord <note>original: HErr</note></term> doth come"; and Revelation 16:15: "Behold, I come as a thief. Blessed is he that watcheth, and keepeth his garments, lest he walk naked, and they see his shame." <note>The author connects the harsh discipline of the Temple guard to the spiritual vigilance commanded in the New Testament.</note>

71) The four chambers which were built in the four corners of the Fire Gate.

72) The Showbread Chamber <term>Chamber of the Makers of the Bread of the Presence <note>Hebrew: לִשְׁכַּת עֹשֵׂי לֶחֶם הַפָּנִים</note></term> in which the showbread was baked in iron molds <term>molds <note>Hebrew: דְּפוּסִים</note></term> weekly, on the day before the Sabbath. They were unleavened, and six pounds of flour went into each one. The expenses for this showbread were taken from the <term>Treasury <note>original Latin: Aerario</note></term> of the Temple, for which the half-<term>shekel <note>original: Seckel</note></term>, which every Israelite gave annually, was also used. These loaves were called <term>Bread of the Presence <note>original Latin: Panes facierum</note></term> <gloss>Showbread</gloss>, or Bread of the Face, because they lay continually before the Most Holy Place and before **God’s** face. Their shape was four-cornered, six spans <note>original: *flache Hände*, "flat hands" or palms</note> long, five spans wide, and one finger thick. At both ends of the length, they had a rim that slanted upwards, and slanted like—

...which descended and was about seven fingers high. When they were baked the day before the Sabbath, they were kept in an oven of moderate warmth until the hour came when they were carried from this **Showbread Chamber** in golden bowls by the priests into the Temple, for they were never to be set out cold, but always warm. For this, the purest wheat or fine flour was always used, and two-tenths <note>A biblical unit of measure, approximately 4.5 liters.</note> or two homers of flour went into each loaf. God willed that throughout His altar and Temple, there should be no honey and no leaven, to indicate that He can tolerate neither hypocrisy nor flattery; therefore, the Showbread also had to be unleavened. It is said that these Showbreads carried a particular power with them, so that if a priest consumed a piece only the size of an olive, he would be completely sated by it. The grain from which the wheat flour and the Showbread were made had to be grown in the land of Judea, and no grain coming from another land could be used for it. The Showbread prefigured Christ, who is the bread of life, which shall feed us and satisfy us unto eternal life. *John 6:48.*

73) The **Lamb Chamber** <gloss>Hebrew: לִשְׁכַּת הַטְּלָאִים, *Lishkat Ha-Tlaim*, meaning "the chamber of the lambs"</gloss>, located toward the West in the inner courtyard, in which eight lambs were constantly kept. Two of these were sacrificed daily, and their place was immediately replaced by two others. Each of them had to stay in this chamber for at least four days so that one could more precisely observe whether it was without blemish, without fault, or infirmity. For Christ, whom all sacrifices prefigured, was to be without blemish—that is, without any sin. *1 Peter 2:22.* Two lambs were sacrificed daily for the entire Israelite people. God Himself ordained this. *Numbers 28:3-4.*

-> These are the offerings / which you shall offer to the Lord / yearling lambs (those under one year old) that are without blemish / two daily for a continual burnt offering; one lamb in the morning / the other between the evenings. <-

The evening sacrifice occurred at the ninth hour of the day, which by our way of reckoning is three o'clock in the afternoon. It is therefore remarkable that on **Good Friday**, at the very same hour when the sacrificial lamb was being slaughtered in the Temple, the Lamb of God was also offered up on the timber of the Cross. For at that very hour, Christ died on the Cross. *Luke 23:44, 46.* And there was a darkness over the whole land until the ninth hour. And Jesus cried out with a loud voice, and said:

# Model

> "Father, I commit my spirit into your hands; and when he had said this, he passed away." <note>A reference to the final words of Jesus in Luke 23:46.</note>

The sacrificial lamb had to be fetched from this Lamb Chamber by a priest. He brought it to the place of slaughter in the area where the 24 iron rings were located, not far from the Altar. There, the Standing Men <note>These were representatives of the people of Israel who attended the sacrifices in shifts.</note>, who came there from the Court of Israel, laid their hands upon the sacrificial lamb in the name of all Israel and confessed the sins of the entire people upon it; thereafter, it was slaughtered by a priest. The blood of the lamb was caught in a golden basin, carried forth, and sprinkled upon the horns of the Altar of Burnt Offering. Then, the sacrificial lamb was laid upon one of the marble tables and divided into pieces; the priests then took the pieces and went in a row, one after another, toward the Altar of Burnt Offering. When they came opposite the door of the Temple, they "heaved" it; that is, they lifted it up in their hands with reverence toward the Temple, as if they wished to offer it to God herewith (from which the German word for sacrifice, *opfern*, originates). This was called a <term>Heave Offering</term> <note>original: *Heb-Opfer*</note>; just as the <term>Wave Offering</term> <note>original: *Webe-Opfer*</note> was "waved" by the priest who carried it in his hands—that is, it was moved toward all four quarters of the world to signify that Christ should be sacrificed for the sins of all people in all four corners of the world. When such waving had occurred, the sacrificial pieces were placed upon the Altar, where they were laid upon the fire and burned to ashes. Through such symbols it was indicated that the blood of the Lamb of God should make us clean from all our sins. Jesus Christ is the Lamb who was slain from the beginning (*Revelation 5:12*); the same was prefigured by each and every sacrificial lamb of the entire Old Testament, and His bloody suffering and death is valid from the first day of the Fall into sin until the final day of the destruction of Heaven and Earth.

74) The **Token Chamber** <note>original: *Zeichen-Cammer*</note>, located toward the east of the gate in the Court of the Gentiles; within it, a "token" or sign was given to those Israelites who desired wine for a drink offering or oil for a grain offering, for which he would afterwards receive what he required in the Oil and Wine Court. Here also were kept the stones from that Altar of Burnt Offering which the Syrians had profaned (*1 Maccabees 4:44, 46*). For this reason, this chamber was also called <gloss>the Chamber of the Altar Stones</gloss> <note>Hebrew: *Lishkat Avnei Mizbe'ach*</note>. For when King Antiochus, that great and powerful enemy of the Jews, came to Jerusalem, he went into the Sanctuary and took away the

<meta>This page continues the description of the Temple's history, transitioning from the sacrificial rituals to the historical desecration by Antiochus Epiphanes and the subsequent restoration of the Altar under the Maccabees, leading into the origins of the Feast of Dedication (Hanukkah).</meta>

golden Altar, Lampstand, and what belonged to it, 1 Maccabees 1:22–23. He also permitted pagan idols to be placed upon God’s Altar, the books of the Law to be torn and burned, and commanded that everyone with whom the books of God's Covenant were found, and all who kept God's Law, should be put to death. v. 57–60. Because the Altar of Burnt Offering was thereby profaned, they deliberated on what was to be done: whether they should let the Altar stand and sanctify it again, or whether it would be better to tear it down completely. They finally chose the latter and tore down the entire Altar, storing the stones on the mountain near the House in a special place until a prophet should come to indicate what should be done with them. 1 Maccabees 4:44–46. However, they took other new, uncut stones according to the Law (Exodus 20:25) and built a new Altar as the previous one had been, and they held the **Dedication of the Altar** <note>original Greek: καὶ ἐποίησαν τὸν ἐγκαινισμὸν τῶ θυσιαστηρίω — "and they performed the dedication of the altar"</note> for eight days, offering burnt offerings and thank offerings upon it with joy. It was also decreed by Judas Maccabeus, his brothers, and the entire assembly of Israel that this Festival of the New Altar should henceforth be kept annually. 1 Maccabees 4:53–59. During this festival, they adorned the Temple with golden crowns and shields, and all the people were very merry. Throughout this festival, no one in the entire land was permitted to fast, nor should any sadness be allowed to take place as long as it lasted; and everyone could celebrate it in their own place, as one was not forced to travel to Jerusalem as was usually required for the high festivals. Every day of the festival, the Great Hallel was sung in the Temple, as was otherwise done during the high festivals—namely, the Psalms of David from 113 to 118 inclusive. In all the houses, lights were burned throughout the night and placed above the house doors, generally numbering as many as the persons dwelling in the house. This Festival of the New Altar is called by the Evangelist John <note>original Greek: τὰ ἐγκαινία — "the dedication"</note> the **Feast of Dedication**. At this Feast of Dedication, Christ preached to the Jews in the Temple. John 10:22. **And it was the Feast of Dedication in Jerusalem / and it was winter / and Jesus walked in the Temple in Solomon's Porch / then the Jews surrounded Him and said to Him: How long do you keep our souls in suspense? / If you are the Christ / tell us plainly.** <note>A direct quotation from the Gospel of John, describing Jesus' presence at the festival commonly known today as Hanukkah.</note> Whereupon the Lord Jesus answered them in love, but they grew embittered and wished to stone Him.

# Model

...was built. In the center of it was a spiral staircase leading underground, by which one descended into the hidden subterranean passages: for the entire mountain of the Temple was vaulted underneath.

76) The Sacrifice Gate <note>Hebrew: *Sha'ar HaKorban*</note>, through which the most holy sacrifices were brought into the inner courtyard and slaughtered on the northern side <note>original: "Mitternachts-Seite," literally "midnight-side," a traditional term for the North</note>. When an Israelite brought an offering, he had to pass through this gate with it, approach the Altar of Burnt Offering, turn his face and the head of the sacrificial animal toward the Temple, lay his hand upon the animal's head, and confess his sins upon it; he then had to draw the sacrificial knife through its throat and thus kill it. There were no more than three kinds of sacrificial animals, namely: cattle, sheep, and goats. All of these were "clean" animals which have split hooves and chew the cud. They are also not of a wild nature, but tame, and could be easily obtained everywhere. The cattle had to be under three years old, and the sheep could not be over two years old, otherwise they were not fit for sacrifice. There were forty-six specific defects counted that made a sacrificial animal unfit to be offered. The Burnt Offering was the most distinguished sacrifice among all, and was so named because all its pieces were completely burned to ashes. These sacrifices altogether were prefigurations <term>types: symbols that foreshadowed future events</term> of the bloody suffering and death of **Jesus Christ**. They all had to be brought to Jerusalem, and no sacrifice was permitted to take place in any other city or location, because Christ was destined to suffer in Jerusalem. The sacrificial animals had to be without blemish—that is, without fault or infirmity—because Christ is without any sin. Sins were laid upon the sacrificial animal with the hands, for all sins were reckoned to Christ before **God’s** judgment. The sacrificial animal had to shed all its blood and die, because Christ also was to shed His blood in His suffering and die a very painful death for the sins of the world. Reconciliation was achieved through the sacrifice, for Christ’s self-sacrifice is the reconciliation for our sins and the sins of the whole world. (1 John 2:2).

77) *Porta Cantûs*, or the Gate of Song, which was so named because it was near the singing and music-making Levites. It was also called *Porta prominentiae* <note>Latin: "Gate of Protrusion"</note> or the Protruding Gate <note>Hebrew: *Sha'ar HaYotze*, "the gate that goes forth"</note>, because it was built out from the wall toward the Court of the Gentiles. Likewise, it was called the Sparkling Flame Gate, because a soldier threw a burning torch from it into the golden windows of the Temple, there-

### of Solomon’s Temple.

...through which, because everything inside was paneled with cedar, it was caught in the flames.

78) That place in this gate where the Levites kept watch. Two walls extended from the wall of the inner courtyard toward the partition wall, one to the right and the other to the left, forming a sort of antechamber <note>original: "Anticameram"</note> which was open toward the north <note>original: "Mitternacht," literally "midnight," a traditional term for the North</note>.

79) The door in the one side-wall, through which one could descend a staircase into the space between the wall of the inner courtyard and the partition wall.

80) The upper chamber in which the priests kept watch <note>Hebrew: *Lishkat Beit HaNitzotz*, meaning the "Chamber of the Spark"</note>. Through the watches in the Temple, spiritual vigilance in faith and a godly life was prefigured <term>prefigured: an Old Testament event or person acting as a symbolic "type" or "shadow" of something in the New Testament</term>. The priests were divided into twenty-four orders by King David. In their thirtieth year, they fully entered the priestly office, and in this they were also a prefiguration of **Jesus Christ** (Luke 3:23). Those priests who were to serve at the Altar had to be without any physical blemish or defect; there were 140 faults and deficiencies that could make a person unfit for the priestly office. Those who presented themselves but could not prove their priestly status from the genealogical registers of the tribe of Levi and the house of Aaron were sent away and rejected by the Sanhedrin <note>original: "Synedrio," the supreme judicial council of the Jews</note>, dressed in a special somber habit <term>habit: a specific style of clothing worn by members of a religious order</term>. However, those who provided sufficient proof were clothed in white garments and admitted into the Priestly Courtyard so that they might serve God in the Temple with the other priests. Christ refers to this when He says:

> Whoever overcomes shall be clothed in white garments, and I will not blot out his name from the Book of Life, but I will confess his name before my Father and before His angels. (Rev. 3:5) <note>original: "Apoc. 3, 5"</note>

Each priestly order began its service on the Sabbath and ended it on the following Sabbath. Everyone knew precisely what his office was and what duties in the sacrificial service pertained to him. When their attendance in the Temple was complete, each could return home from Jerusalem. The priestly watches were a prefiguration of the spiritual priests of the New Testament—which all Christians are meant to be. Their duty is to carefully guard the temple of their heart so that nothing unclean enters, and so that everything within the service of God proceeds in an orderly manner.

81) The Washing Chamber, in which the pieces of the sacrifice, before one—

...that they were washed clean and pure before being brought to the altar. God the Lord alludes to this by telling the Jews through the prophet Isaiah: "Wash yourselves, make yourselves clean; put away your evil nature from before my eyes; cease to do evil, learn to do good." <note>Isaiah 1:16</note>

82) **The Parvah Chamber** <note>Hebrew: *Lishkat Beit ha-Parvah*</note> in which the hides of the sacrificial animals were stored and sprinkled with salt.

83) **The Exit of the Hidden Passage** beneath the earth, through which the skins and hides of the sacrificial animals were brought out into the Court of the Gentiles and transported further away.

84) **The Salt Chamber** <note>Hebrew: *Lishkat ha-Melach*</note>, where all the salt used in the Temple was kept.

85) **The Outer Court, also called the Women’s Court** <note>Hebrew: *Ezrat Nashim*</note>, because they were permitted to go that far, but no further. Regarding these locations, eleven distinct <term>gradus</term> <note>Latin: "steps" or "degrees"</note> and levels of holiness were observed among the Jews:

I. The Land of Israel was considered holier than all other lands in the whole world.
II. The cities in the Land of Israel surrounded by walls were held to be holier than the rest of the Jewish land. For this reason, no dead person was buried within those walls.
III. The city of Jerusalem was holier than other cities.
IV. The Mountain of the House of the Lord, otherwise called the Court of the Gentiles, was holier than the city of Jerusalem.
V. The space between the partition wall and the wall of the Temple was holier than the Mountain of the House or the Court of the Gentiles.
VI. The Women’s Court was holier than the space between the wall and the partition wall.
VII. The Court of Israel was holier than the Women’s Court.
VIII. The Priests’ Court was holier than the Court of Israel.
IX. The space between the Temple and the Altar was holier than the Priests’ Court.
X. The Temple itself was holier than the space between the Temple and the Altar.
XI. The Most Holy Place, however, surpassed all places in the Jewish land and in the entire world in holiness.

86) **The steps to the Outer Court, which was 6 cubits <note>original: "Ellen"</note> higher than the Court of the Gentiles.**

87) **The 32 steps to the Inner Court, which was 16 cubits <note>original: "Ellen"</note> higher than the Court of the Gentiles.** This is why Scripture speaks of "going up" into the Temple; every Israelite, through the physical lifting of his body, was meant to remember the lifting of his heart to God. <note>Lamentations 3:41</note>

### Of Solomon's Temple.

88) **The Women's Gate**, which led into the outer court on the North side. <note>The original German uses "Mitternacht" (Midnight) to denote the North.</note>

89) **The Women's Gate** on the South side. <note>The original German uses "Mittag" (Midday) to denote the South.</note>

90) **The Women's Galleries**, featuring lattice-work standing upon the railings, where they attended the divine services in the Temple. It was here that the pious **Anna** was very often and daily present; for she never left the Temple, serving God with fasting and prayer night and day. *Luke 2:37.*

91) **The Columns and Halls** in the outer court on three sides: toward the East, South, and North. <note>The original German uses "Morgen" (Morning) for East, "Mittag" (Midday) for South, and "Mitternacht" (Midnight) for North.</note>

92) **The thirteen alms boxes** <note>Hebrew: שופרות, *shofarot*, so-called because they were shaped like trumpets—narrow at the top and wide at the bottom—to prevent theft.</note> near the columns. Upon each of these "God-chests" was written the purpose for which the contribution would be used. What was placed into these God-chests was called **Corban**, a gift dedicated and honored to God. In this regard, many sins were eventually committed. For if there were poor parents who had a son who had come into good fortune, and they asked him for support, but he did not wish to help them, he would say: **"Corban"**—that which I could have used to help you, I have dedicated as a gift to God in the Temple. Through this, it happened that many poor parents were abandoned in an unjust and unmerciful manner. Because the Pharisees nonetheless approved of and highly praised this, **Christ** severely rebuked them for it. *Matthew 15:4, 5, 6.* God commanded: *Honor your father and mother; but whoever curses father or mother shall die the death.* But you teach: *Whoever speaks to father or mother: "Whatever help you might have otherwise received from me is a gift to God," he does well.* <note>The text paraphrases the biblical argument where religious vows were used to circumvent the duty of caring for parents.</note> By this, it happens that henceforth no one honors his father and mother, and you have thus nullified God's commandment for the sake of your own traditions. Legitimate, willing alms were pleasing to God, and He gave great promises to generous Israelites. Also regarding the Tabernacle and the Temple, He commanded in *Exodus 23:14, 15*: *Three times a year you shall hold a festival for me. But do not appear before me empty-handed;* that is, without a gift or without a sacrifice. Thus, no one easily entered the Temple without putting something in, however little it might be. It was at these God-chests that the **Lord Jesus** saw the poor widow who placed two mites into the God-chest. *Luke 21:1–4.* *He looked up and saw the rich as they placed their offerings into the God-chest. He also saw*

# of Solomon's Temple.

having received a special, remarkable benefit, he considered himself obligated to bring a **Thanksgiving Offering** to **GOD** for it. However, he did not always go immediately into the Temple with his sacrifice, but sanctified himself beforehand for many days—usually 30 days—during which he separated himself from the common way of living. He prayed diligently, devoutly meditated on **GOD’s** word, avoided worldly pleasures, let his hair grow freely, drank no wine nor consumed anything that came from the grapevine, and did not go near any dead person. When the 30 days were over, the Nazarite <note>From the Hebrew *nazir*, meaning "consecrated" or "separated." A person who took a voluntary vow of holiness.</note> then came into the Court of the Nazarites, had his hair cut off, threw it into the fire, and brought his sacrifice to the **LORD**. Numbers 6:1–21. Such Nazarites were a prefiguration of **Christ**, who was separated from sinners (Hebrews 7:26) and was not of this world (John 8:23), and also of Christians, who are betrothed to **GOD** because of their baptismal vows.

99) The Wood Court <note>original Hebrew: לשכת דיר העצים, *Lishkat Dir Ha-Etzim*</note> in the northeast <note>literally the "morning and midnight" corner</note> corner, in which all the wood that was to be brought to the Altar of Burnt Offering was selected. All wood that was crooked, knotty, decaying, or worm-eaten was considered unfit for the altar. In particular, no wood from the grapevine or the olive tree was allowed to come upon the Altar of Burnt Offering, because the oil from the olive tree was already used in the grain offering <term>Speiß-Opfer; a sacrifice of flour mixed with oil</term> and the wine from the grapevine was already used in the drink offering <term>Tranck-Opfer; a libation of wine poured out</term> on the altar. However, the crooked and knotty wood could still be used elsewhere in the Temple for the fire in which the thanksgiving offerings were cooked. Likewise, it was burned in the Fire Gate, where there was a priestly watch and where a fire was constantly maintained. Priests with physical defects, who were not permitted to serve at the altar, were required to select the wood here and separate the fit from the unfit. There were specifically nine days in the year designated for this purpose, on which the wood had to be brought and stored in the Temple. Also, nine priestly families were assigned to this, having been specifically charged with the responsibility of caring for it. As soon as the wood was brought into the Temple, it was placed in this Wood Court. But that which was now selected and fit for the altar was brought southward into the Fire Gate, where there was a <term>Conclave Ligni</term> <note>Latin: "Wood Chamber"</note> and a storage area where the wood was placed so that it would not be far from the altar and would be right at hand. For **GOD** had commanded that they should never let the fire on the altar go out. Leviticus 6:12, 13. The fire on the altar (which was ignited from heaven at the first sacrifice of Aaron) shall burn

[of the bird] that was slaughtered, dipped with cedar wood, scarlet wool <note>original: "Rosinfarbener Wolle"</note>, and hyssop, and the person who had been leprous was sprinkled with it. Thereupon, the living bird was allowed to fly into the open field, and the purified person had to wash his clothes, shave off all his hair, and bathe himself with water; thus he was clean and was allowed to go in and out of the camp again, yet he still had to remain outside his tent for seven days, and on the eighth day bring his sacrifice. Within the Temple, the purification of a leper occurred in the following manner. He came into the Temple in the early morning, specifically into the <term>Court of the Lepers</term> <note>A designated area in the corner of the Women's Court where those formerly afflicted with skin diseases underwent ritual cleansing</note>, where he bathed and washed himself with water. Thereupon, the Captain of the Temple came and led the one who had been leprous, along with the others who were to be purified, from the outer court up the rounded steps into the <term>Nicanor Gate</term> <note>The great bronze gate that served as the main entrance to the inner Court of Israel</note>; there he had to remain standing, facing the door of the Temple beneath the gate, for he was not yet permitted to enter the inner court. A priest then came and accepted his sacrifice there, and waved it before the Lord; he also had the person who had been leprous place his hands—which he stretched out through the gate—upon the head of the sacrificial animal. He then slaughtered it at the place of slaughter and subsequently moistened the cartilage of the person’s right ear, the thumb of the right hand, and the big toe of the right foot with the blood. Furthermore, the priest dipped his finger into the oil and moistened those same parts on top of the blood. Finally, his sin and guilt offering was sacrificed upon the altar, and he was then declared clean. After this, he entered the inner court to join the rest of the Israelites, ate of the thank-offering in the <term>dining halls</term> <note>original: "Esse-Saalen"</note> with his friends, and at last went down again to his house to his own people, from whom he had been separated for a long time, now joyful over the goodness God had shown him. It was into this Court of the Lepers that the same man who had been leprous came—the one whom the Lord **JESUS** made whole and commanded to show himself to the priests and offer the gift and sacrifice that **Moses** had commanded. The ten lepers also came into this court, who met the Lord **JESUS** on His journey toward Jerusalem, lifted up their voices, and said:

> **Jesus**, dear Master, have mercy on us.

When He saw them, He spoke to them: "Go and show yourselves to the priests."

In the Temple, the purification of a leper occurred in the following manner. He came early in the morning to the Temple, specifically to the **Court of the Lepers**, and there bathed and washed himself with water. Thereupon, the Captain of the Temple came and led the one who had been leprous, along with the others who were to be cleansed, out from the outer court up the rounded steps into the **Gate of Nicanor** <note>These were the fifteen semi-circular steps leading from the Women's Court to the Israelite Court</note>; there he had to remain standing toward the door of the Temple beneath the gate, for he was not yet permitted to enter into the inner court. 

However, a priest came and received his sacrifice there, waved it before the Lord, and had the one who had been leprous place his hands—which he stretched out through the door—upon the head of the sacrificial animal. [The priest] slaughtered it at the place of slaughter and subsequently moistened the man’s right ear at the bottom of the cartilage, the thumb of the right hand, and the big toe of the right foot with the blood. Furthermore, the priest dipped his finger into the oil and moistened those same parts on top of the blood. Finally, his sin and guilt offering was sacrificed upon the altar, and he was then declared clean. Afterward, he entered the inner court to join the rest of the Israelites, ate of the thank offering in the dining halls <note>original: "Esse-Saalen"</note> with his friends, and finally went back down to his home and his family, from whom he had been separated for a long time, now joyful over the goodness shown by God. 

It was into this Court of the Lepers that the same man who had been leprous came, whom the Lord JESUS had made well and commanded that he should show himself to the priests and offer the gift and sacrifice that Moses commanded. The ten lepers also came into this court, who met the Lord Jesus on the journey toward Jerusalem, lifted up their voices, and said: "Jesus, dear Master, have mercy on us!" When He saw them, He said to them: "Go and show yourselves to the priests."

<meta>This page continues the numbered description of the Temple model's various courts and ritual objects, specifically focusing on the storage of offerings and the ceremonies of the Day of Atonement and the Feast of Tabernacles.</meta>

# Model

And it happened that as they went (toward Jerusalem), they were cleansed, so that the priests in the Temple had to declare them perfectly healthy and completely purified from leprosy. Luke 17:11, 14. <note>original: "Luc. 17, 11. 14."</note>

101) The **Wine and Oil Court** <note>Hebrew original: לשׁכת בית שׁמניא (*Lishkat Beit Shemanya*), meaning the "Chamber of the Oil House"</note> where the wine for the daily drink offering and the oil, which was used for the grain offerings and for the golden lampstands in the Temple, was stored. Leviticus 2:1. Exodus 27:20.

102) The **raised seat or pulpit** <note>Hebrew original: בימה שׁل עץ (*Bimah shel etz*), meaning "wooden platform"</note> upon which the High Priest, on the Day of Atonement, had to read several passages from the Law of Moses. Namely, the sixteenth chapter of the third book of Moses <note>Leviticus</note>, and then from the twenty-third chapter, verses 27, 28, 29, 30, 31, and 32. Likewise, from the fourth book of Moses <note>Numbers</note>, the 29th chapter, verses 7, 8, 9, and 10. All of which deal with the Day of Atonement. For as soon as it was known in Jerusalem and in the Temple that the **scapegoat** <note>original: *Versöhn-Bock*</note> had arrived in the wilderness and had been cast down the rock and killed there, the High Priest went into the outer courtyard to this pulpit or preaching-chair, read the aforementioned passages from the Law of Moses, and then offered eight prayers concerning: 1. the Law, 2. the Divine Service, 3. the Praise of God, 4. the Forgiveness of Sins, 5. the Sanctuary, 6. the Children of Israel, 7. the Priests, and 8. other people. There he devoutly called upon God, asking that He would continue to grant them His Law, dwell constantly with them in the Temple, graciously accept their sacrifices, and protect them against all enemies.

103) The **large golden lampstands in the outer courtyard**, each of which had four golden lamps that were lit in the evening during the joy of the **Feast of Tabernacles** <note>original: *Lauber-Hütten-Fest*</note>, which lasted late into the night. These golden lampstands were lit in the middle of the outer courtyard at nightfall. Because they were quite high, there was a staircase leading up to each lampstand. Oil was poured into the four golden lamps, but the wicks for them were made from threads of discarded priestly garments. Because there were many lampstands and the flames on the lamps were made quite large, they provided such a bright light that not only was the entire outer courtyard fully illuminated, but the glow could be perceived everywhere in the streets of the city of Jerusalem.

104) The **Tabernacles** <note>or "booths"</note> which were set up in the Temple for the priests and Levites during this festival, which lasted 7 days. Leviticus 23:34. To build these tabernacles, branches from "fat trees," such as olive trees and the like, were used, so that they

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the ritual practices during the Feast of Tabernacles, detailing the construction of booths and the specific liturgy performed in the Temple courts.</meta>

->of Solomon's Temple.<-

they remained green for the entire seven days. **The booths were built under** the open sky, on the streets, in the courtyards, and upon the roofs of their houses. At this Feast of Tabernacles <note>original: *Lauber-Hütten-Fest*, better known today as Sukkot</note>, all males were required to be present in Jerusalem and at the Temple; therefore, many booths were also built outside the ring-walls of the city of Jerusalem. However, they were not permitted to be further from the city than a Sabbath day's journey <note>A traditional Jewish limit for travel on holy days, approximately 2,000 cubits or about half a mile</note>. In the courtyards of the Temple, the walls within the archways were hung with beautiful, colorful tapestries. On the first day of the festival, the Great Hallel <note>A series of celebratory Psalms (113–118) sung on major festivals</note> was sung, and besides that, a special Psalm was sung every day throughout the entire festival. On the first day, the 105th Psalm was sung: **Give thanks to the Lord, and preach His name.** On the second day, the 29th Psalm: **Ascribe unto the Lord, O ye mighty, ascribe unto the Lord glory and strength.** On the third day, the 50th Psalm, verses 1–16: **God the Lord, the Mighty One, speaks and calls the world from the rising of the sun to its setting.** On the fourth day, the 94th Psalm, verses 1–16: **O Lord God, to whom vengeance belongeth; O God, to whom vengeance belongeth, show Thyself.** On the fifth day, the 95th Psalm, verses 1–8: **O come, let us sing unto the Lord; let us make a joyful noise to the rock of our salvation.** On the sixth day, the 81st Psalm, verses 1–6: **Sing aloud unto God our strength.** On the seventh day, the 82nd Psalm, verses 1–5: **God standeth in the congregation of God.** During the Psalms and songs of praise, which the High Priest and the most prominent of the people sang in the outer courtyard, the Levites played their harps, psalteries, and cymbals. The Israelites were required to live in such booths for the full seven days, which was quite difficult for them because the festival fell in autumn—when day and night are equal—and the weather is usually cold and rainy. In doing so, they were to remember that their ancestors lived in such booths for 40 years in the wilderness, and they were to thank God that they, by contrast, could now live in well-built houses in the Promised Land. They were also to remember that, despite this, they were still strangers and pilgrims in this world, and would eventually have to leave their houses and all that they possessed. **For here we have no continuing city, but we seek one to come. Hebrews 13:14.**

105) The **Music Chambers of the Levites** <note>Hebrew original: לשכות כלי שיר (*Lishkot Klei Shir*), meaning "Chambers of the musical instruments"</note>, which were 7 cubits high and had doors that opened into the outer courtyard.

<meta>This page continues the description of the Temple's architectural features and their ritual uses, specifically focusing on the musical practices of the Levites and the significance of the internal gates.</meta>

# Model

...rooms. In these, the Levites stored their musical instruments and also practiced their music.

106) The fifteen steps / built as a half-circle <note>original Hebrew: *ma'alot agulot kema'aseh chatzi goren*, "rounded steps like the shape of a half-threshing floor"; Latin: *gradus rotundi veluti dimidius circulus*, "round steps like a half circle"</note>, upon which the fifteen Psalms of Ascent <note>Psalms 120 through 134</note> were sung by the Levites during the Feast of Tabernacles <note>Sukkot</note>. The 120th Psalm was sung on the top step, the 121st on the following step, and so forth. Regarding these "Psalms of the Steps" <note>original: *Psalmos graduum*</note>, Luther always uses the heading **A song in the higher choir** in his translation. Some doubt whether King David actually intended these Psalms for such steps, since the Temple had not yet been built at that time. However, since King David gave the complete blueprint of the Temple to Solomon—in which such an important part of the entire building as the ascent from the outer court into the inner court (which was seven and a half cubits high) would not have been missing—and since he himself regulated the orders of the priests and Levites, it is reasonable to assume that in these "Psalms of the Steps" he also had in mind those very steps upon which they were subsequently and permanently sung.

107) The Gate of Nicanor <note>Hebrew: *Sha’ar Nicanor*</note>, which was also called the New Gate <note>Hebrew: *Sha’ar He-Chadash*</note> and the Bronze Gate <note>Hebrew: *Sha’ar Ha-Nechoshet*</note>. One passed through this gate from the outer court into the inner court; it was made of bronze and was so heavy that twenty men were required to heave it open. This is the very gate that opened by itself one night before the destruction of the Temple <note>A famous omen recorded by the historian Josephus</note>. Those of a bold and reckless mind said: "This is a good sign; God has opened the door to all good things for us." But those who sighed over the sins and abominations occurring in the city of Jerusalem and throughout the entire land said: "No, this is not a good omen; rather, God will open the gates to our enemies"—which indeed later came to pass. This gate received its name either from the man who built it, or from the wicked Nicanor. The latter, having forced his way into the city of Jerusalem with his army, entered the Temple, stretched out his hand against the sanctuary and said to the priests who were present: "If you do not deliver Judas <note>Judas Maccabeus</note> and his army into my hand, I will burn this house with fire." (1 Maccabees 7:35; 2 Maccabees 14:32). But when God gave the Jews victory over him, his corpse was found on the battlefield, and his hand was cut off and later hung up facing the Temple. (2 Maccabees 15:33).

<meta>This page continues the description of the Gate of Nicanor, specifically detailing its daily ritual use and its role as the seat of the Jewish judicial system.</meta>

# Of Solomon's Temple.

108) The two doors of the **Gate of Nicanor** <note>original Latin: *Nicanoris*</note> located in both gate-wings, which were opened daily, whereas the entire gate was only opened on the Sabbath, New Moons, and high festivals. It was to this point that women after childbirth <note>original: *Sechswöchnerinnen*, referring to the traditional forty-day period of purification after birth</note> had to come for their purification when they presented their children to the **Lord**; and it was here that the Holy Virgin **Mary** presented the **Infant Jesus** to the **Lord**. Luke 2:22. She brought her offering according to the command of **God**. Leviticus 12:6. The wealthy brought a year-old lamb as a burnt offering; but for the sake of the poor, **God** had given such a law: "If she cannot afford a sheep, she shall take two turtledoves or two young pigeons, one for a burnt offering and the other for a sin offering." Leviticus 12:8. Now, while the Holy Virgin **Mary** with the **Infant Jesus** stands in these doors of the **Gate of Nicanor** and presents Him to the **Lord**, the elderly man, Simeon, comes into the Temple into the **Court of the Israelites** <note>original: *Vorhof Israelis*</note> by the prompting of the Spirit. An answer had been given to him by the Holy Spirit that he should not see death until he had first seen the **Christ** of the **Lord**. And now he saw the **Christ** of the **Lord**, went there full of joy in his heart, took the **Infant Jesus** from the arms of the Holy Virgin **Mary** into his own, and said:

> **Lord**, eternal **God**, now I will gladly die, after I have seen the **Savior** of all the world, **Jesus Christ**. **Lord**, now lettest thou thy servant depart in peace, according to thy word; for mine eyes have seen thy **Savior**, which thou hast prepared before the face of all people; a light to lighten the Gentiles, and for the glory of thy people **Israel**. Luke 2:22–32.

109) The chamber above the **Gate of Nicanor**, in which the **Small Sanhedrin** <note>Hebrew: סַנְהֶדְרִין קְטַנָּה, *Sanhedrin Ketannah*</note> sat, which consisted of 23 persons. There were three kinds of **Courts** <note>original Latin: *Judicia*</note> among the Jews. The High Court or **Sanhedrin** <note>original: *Synedrium*</note>, consisting of 71 persons; the Middle Court, consisting of 23 persons; and the Lower Court, which consisted of only three persons. All three types were present in the Temple: the Lower Court was in the chamber above the Mountain Gate <note>the gate leading to the Temple Mount</note> of the Temple; the Middle Court was above the **Gate of Nicanor**; and the High Court was held in the uppermost Courtroom, located in the Temple toward the south. Whatever could not be settled by the Lower Court was brought before the Middle Court, and those matters which could not be decided here were allowed to reach the High **Sanhedrin**. One had to abide by its **ruling** <note>original Latin: *deciso*</note>, and whoever contradicted the High Court had to [suffer] death.

# Model

...shall surely die. Deuteronomy 17:12, 13. And if anyone acts presumptuously, so as not to obey the priest who stands there in the office of the **LORD** your **GOD**, or the judge, that person shall die; and you shall put away the evil from Israel, that all the people may hear and fear, and no longer act presumptuously.

110) The **Inner Court** <term>the inner court <note>Hebrew: *he-chatzer ha-p'nimit*</note></term> was surrounded by walls on all sides; on the southern and northern sides, it was built up with rooms, chambers, halls, and walkways, and it was paved with marble. This inner court had various divisions: for it included the Court of the Israelites, the Court of the Priests, the place of slaughter, the space between the Temple and the Altar, and the area surrounding the Temple itself. Women were not permitted to enter this court; only male Israelites and those who had converted to the Jewish religion—known as **proselytes** <note>original: *Jüden-Genossen*, referring to "righteous proselytes" or *ger tzedek*</note>—could enter. These individuals had previously been heathens and had abandoned paganism out of love for **God**, thereby attaining all the rights and freedoms of natural-born Israelites. However, they were not permitted to serve in public offices, nor could they sit on the High Council <note>the Sanhedrin</note>, nor command or preside over natural-born Israelites.

111) The **Court of the Israelites** <term>the court of Israel <note>Hebrew: *ezrat yisra’el*</note></term> was eleven cubits wide and 135 cubits long.

112) The **24 Israelites**, who were called the **Representative Men** <term>men of the standing <note>Hebrew: *anshei ma’amad*</note></term>, were required to be present in the Court of the Israelites daily during the divine service on behalf of the entire nation. The court took its name from these Israelites, and not from the whole people of Israel, of whom only a small portion could be present at any one time. They arrived at the Temple the evening before so that they would be present immediately at the start of the service the following morning. There were two from every tribe, making twenty-four in total. They lived either in Jerusalem or very near to it. Their service and attendance in the Temple always lasted for only one week, after which others took their place. They were required to observe the sacrifices while standing, as no one was permitted to sit anywhere within the inner court. When the week came to an end and they were ready to return home, they were always dismissed with the following blessing:

> May He who dwells in this house plant among you brotherhood, love, peace, and friendship.

113) The **Throne of King Solomon** <term>the royal throne <note>Hebrew: *kisse ha-malkhut*</note></term> in the court

the Court of Israel, 3 cubits high and 5 cubits wide, standing upon a marble column, upon which Solomon prayed at the dedication of the Temple and spread his hands toward heaven, when **God** answered his prayer with fire from heaven. 2 Chronicles 6:13; 7:1. This fire is described as not being like ordinary fire that separates into flickering flames, but rather it was a very bright radiance and rays, more beautiful than the sun, which one could look at steadily with open eyes without them being harmed in the least. Such a fiery glow is called in Holy Scripture **the Glory of the LORD**. 

At the dedication of the Temple, an innumerable multitude of people from across the entire land of Israel was present. 1 Kings 8:1, 2. Then King Solomon gathered to himself the elders of Israel / all the heads of the tribes and the princes of the fathers among the children of Israel / to Jerusalem / to bring up the **Ark of the Covenant of the LORD** (into the Temple) out of the City of David / which is Zion / (where it had previously stood in the Tabernacle of Moses). When Solomon had finished praying / a fire fell from heaven and consumed the burnt offering and other sacrifices. All the children of Israel also saw the fire fall down / and the **Glory of the LORD** above the house / and they fell on their knees with their faces to the earth upon the pavement / and worshipped / and thanked the **LORD** / that He is good and His mercy endures forever. The King, however, and all the people offered sacrifices to the **LORD**. This bright radiance and fire, after it had consumed the sacrifices, hovered over the house of the **Lord**, then moved down into the house, and finally remained constantly within the pillar of cloud and fire.

116) The Chamber of Gemariah <note>original Hebrew: *lishkat Gemaryahu ben-Shaphan*</note> in this same wall, wherein Baruch—the scribe of the prophet Jeremiah—had to read the speeches of Jeremiah which he had written down from his mouth, because Jeremiah himself was in prison. 117) The Vestry <term>German: *Kleider-Cammer*</term> / or the Chamber of Phinehas <note>original Hebrew: *lishkat Pinehas*</note> to the right of the Gate of Nicanor, in which the holy garments of the priests were kept in 96 chests. This chamber was divided internally into various other rooms, and on each chest the name of the garments kept inside was written. There was also a specific Keeper of the Garments appointed for this, who helped the priests dress and undress. One such man was Shallum, the husband of the prophetess Huldah, whom King Josiah of Judah sent to seek counsel because of the rediscovered original Book of the Law of Moses and the threats contained within it. 2 Chronicles 34:22. And because one particular Keeper of the Garments had held this office for a very long time, whose name was Phinehas, the Vestry where he constantly performed his duties was called the Chamber of Phinehas. The priests always went in snow-white garments and girded themselves with a belt. There were 24 priestly divisions <term>also known as "courses" or "orders"</term>. Each of these had 4 chests in which the garments lay that they had to put on for their official duties. <meta>This page continues the detailed inventory of the rooms within the Temple complex, transitioning from the symbolic meaning of the priestly garments mentioned at the end of page 50 to the specific function of the chamber used for preparing the High Priest's daily offerings and the seasonal Wave-Sheaf.</meta>

->of Solomon’s Temple.<-

...but always in the garments of salvation, and in the robe of the righteousness of **JESUS CHRIST**. Isaiah 61:10.

118) The specific chamber in which the High Priest’s daily grain offering <term>German: *Speiß-Opfer* — a non-animal sacrifice usually consisting of flour, oil, and incense</term> was baked <note>original Hebrew: *lishkat osei chavitin* — the Chamber of the Makers of the Flat-Cakes</note>. Whenever the daily grain offering was sacrificed on behalf of the entire people, the High Priest was also required to have a grain offering sacrificed for himself. For this, a tenth part of an ephah <term>Ephah: an ancient Hebrew unit of dry volume, approximately 22 liters</term> of wheat flour and oil were used, mixed together, and divided into two parts. Each part was further divided into six small cakes, so that there were twelve in total. Then it was placed in a pan over the fire and baked. The first six cakes were sacrificed in the morning, and the other six in the evening. However, each such small cake was always broken again into two pieces, so that there were twelve pieces offered in the morning and twelve in the evening. To this, a handful of frankincense was always added, and this grain offering of the High Priest was completely burned upon the altar, so that no one was permitted to eat any of it. Leviticus 6:19–23. *Every grain offering of a priest shall be entirely burned; it shall not be eaten*, whereas, by contrast, only a handful of an ordinary Israelite’s grain offering was sacrificed, and the remainder belonged to the priest. The High Priest had to have such grain offerings sacrificed daily for himself at his own expense. A grain offering for the entire people, however, included among others the Wave-Sheaf <term>German: *Webe-Garbe* — an offering of the first fruits of the harvest</term>, which was sacrificed on the second day of Passover <note>original: *Oster-Feyertag* — while the text uses the German word for Easter, in this historical context it refers to the Jewish Passover</note> and was prepared in this Baker’s Chamber. Certain persons were appointed by the Sanhedrin <note>original: *Synedrio* — the supreme council and court of justice in ancient Israel</note> who went out into the field on the first day of Passover as soon as the sun had set and cut as much barley as constituted a sheaf. For at this time in the land of the Jews—which lay further south than our lands—the fruits of the field and the grain were already reaching their maturity. The cut barley was brought into the Temple, where the ears of barley were dried by the fire, the grains beaten out, and then ground into flour. One omer <term>Omer: a unit of measure equal to about 2 liters or one-tenth of an ephah</term> of such flour was taken, mixed with oil, placed in a golden vessel, and handed over to the priest with a handful of frankincense on top. He waved it before the **LORD** toward the Temple, brought it most reverently to the altar, salted it with salt, and threw the frankincense and a handful of such barley dough into the fire, thus sacrificing it to the **LORD**. The rest, which he brought back down, belonged to him and was his portion. This

<meta>This page continues the catalog of specific locations and functional areas within the Temple complex. It concludes the description of the Wave-Sheaf and moves into a numbered list of chambers and architectural features, focusing on the roles of the Levites and the physical boundaries between the sacred courtyards.</meta>

# Model

The **<term>Wave-Sheaf</term>** <note>German: *Web-Garbe*; Hebrew: *Omer*</note> was the firstfruits of the entire harvest, and no one was permitted to harvest any grain in the field until this Wave-Sheaf had been offered in the Temple.

119) **The Chamber of Nathan-Melech** <note>Hebrew: *lishkat Nethan-Melech*</note>, where certain idolatrous kings of Judah, following the Persian custom, kept chariots and horses in honor of the sun. These were burned with fire by the pious King of Judah, Josiah, who thereby abolished and exterminated the idolatry. 2 Kings 23:11.

120) **The <term>Stone Chamber</term>** <note>Hebrew: *lishkat beit ha-even*</note>, located in the corner of the inner courtyard toward the north and east <note>original: *Mitternacht und Morgen*</note>, in which all marble and earthenware vessels used in the Temple were kept.

121) **The <term>Paling Fence</term>** <note>German: *Stacket-Werck*</note> **in the Courtyard of the Israelites**, placed there so that the Israelites would not proceed any further, and might not push from the Courtyard of the Israelites into the Priests' Courtyard, which was an area they were not permitted to enter.

122) **The 4 Steps from the Courtyard of the Israelites** into the Priests' Courtyard, where the first step was one cubit high and one cubit wide, while the other three were each half a cubit high and wide.

123) **The Pulpit and proper place of the Levites**, where they stood when they performed music in the Temple. The Levites descended from Levi, the third son of the patriarch Jacob. These were given by God to the priests as assistants in the divine service. Numbers 3:9: *And thou shalt give the Levites unto Aaron and to his sons as a gift from the children of Israel.* It would have been right for all the firstborn in Israel to serve God in the Temple and assist the priests, for God had sanctified all the firstborn to Himself. But He accepted the tribe of Levi in place of the firstborn. Numbers 3:12: *Behold, I have taken the Levites from among the children of Israel instead of all the firstborn that openeth the matrix among the children of Israel: therefore the Levites shall be mine.* Near the <term>Tabernacle</term> <note>German: *Stifts-Hütte* — the portable sanctuary used by the Israelites in the wilderness</note>, they had their camp closest to the structure. They were required to set up the Tabernacle and take it apart again, and also to carry the holy vessels during travel. Numbers 1:50: *But thou shalt appoint the Levites over the tabernacle of testimony, and over all the vessels thereof, and over all things that belong to it: they shall bear the tabernacle, and all the vessels thereof; and they shall minister unto it, and shall encamp round about the tabernacle.* In the time of David and later in the Temple, the [service] of the Le-

->of the Temple of Solomon.<-

...Levites <note>The text begins mid-word, "viten," continuing "Leviten" from the previous page.</note> began their preparation for service as early as their twentieth year. 1 Chronicles 23:24. And when they had served until their fiftieth year, they were then freed from the most difficult duties. Numbers 8:25, 26. However, before their thirtieth year, none were fully admitted to the office, even though they were prepared for it from the twentieth year. When King David wished to establish an order among the Levites and had them counted, he found there were 38,000 of them in number. He divided these into four parts, and appointed twenty-four thousand for the divine service, six thousand as judges and officials, four thousand as gatekeepers, and four thousand as singers of praise. 1 Chronicles 24:4. The first among these were named <note>distinguished</note> above all other Levites. They stood under the hand and supervision of the priests and had to care for the holy vessels, basins, bowls, fire-pans, and other implements of the Temple, handing them to the priests, taking them back from them, returning them to their proper places, and delivering them to the overseers. They wore white linen tunics, and it is said to the glory of the tribe of Levi that they always remained steadfast to GOD, even when other Israelites from the remaining tribes fell into idolatry and great sins.

124) **The Choir of the Levites**, consisting of 12 singers at the **Altar of Burnt Offering**. There could never be fewer than 12, but there could be as many more as one desired. The number of the regular 12 singers was a reference to the 12 tribes of Israel. On high festivals, when all males from the entire Promised Land had to appear, the 4,000 Levites appointed by King David for singing and music during the divine service were also always present. GOD bestowed upon them the necessary special gifts for this; they also had a strong inclination toward it and devoted their entire lives primarily to cultivating <note>original: *excolireten*</note> music to the best of their ability according to the customs of the time. Therefore, the most magnificent music was found in the Temple in Jerusalem. Those who had a harsh voice were not permitted to appear at the pulpit of the Levites. The regular place where the singers stood was toward the east <note>original: *gegen Morgen*; literally "toward the morning"</note> of the altar, on the steps by which one came from the Courtyard of the Israelites into the Priests' Courtyard. Just as no "strange fire" could be brought onto the altar by the priests, so too no "strange songs"—aside from the Psalms of David—could be sung in the Temple by the Levites. The book of their songs was the Book of Psalms. 2 Samuel 23:1. Their service in the Temple began in their twentieth year, and in their fiftieth year

<meta>This page continues the description of the Temple's musical service, transitioning from the organization of the Levite singers to a detailed examination of the specific instruments they used, beginning with the harp and the psaltery.</meta>

# Model

The harp must have already been known in the times of the Patriarch Jacob, although the shape common in those days is unknown. Laban said to Jacob, when the latter had secretly departed from him:
> Why did you not tell me, so that I might have sent you away with joy, with singing, with drums <note>original: "Pausen," referring to timbrels or percussion</note> and harps?
Genesis 31:27. The harp had a pleasant sound to gladden the sorrowful. 1 Samuel 16:23.
> Whenever the spirit of GOD came upon Saul, David took the harp and played with his hand; thus Saul was refreshed and became better, and the evil spirit departed from him.
The harp, as well as other musical instruments, was often abused by the godless for sinful worldly pleasure. The Prophet Isaiah complains of this in chapter 5, verse 12:
> They have harps, psalteries, drums, pipes, and wine in their luxury, and do not look upon the work of the LORD, nor behold the deeds of His hands.
However, this instrument was primarily dedicated to divine service; therefore, the 147th Psalm, verse 7, encourages the Levites:
> Sing to one another to the LORD, and praise our GOD with harps.
Hence, the praise and glory of GOD in eternal life is represented under the image of such musical instruments in Revelation 15:2–3. Those who had gained victory over the beast held the harps of GOD and sang the song of Moses, the servant of GOD, and the song of the Lamb, saying:
> Great and wondrous are Your works, LORD God Almighty; righteous and true are Your ways, You King of Saints.

127) The psalteries <term>the lyres <note>original Hebrew: הַקִּנּוֹרוֹת (ha-kinnorot)</note></term>, which were in the style of our lutes, and of which two were always present during the daily divine service. On greater solemnities, however, there were at most six psalteries. The holy Apostle Paul refers to this when he says: Sing and play <term>make melody <note>original Greek: ψάλλητε (psallēte)</note></term> to the Lord in your hearts. Ephesians 5:19. The small psaltery had only three strings, yet one could produce twelve tones <note>original: "tonos"</note> upon it; the large psaltery, however, had ten strings, but in such a way that they were always strung in pairs, so that two and two together gave one tone. King David mentions this in the 33rd Psalm, verse 2:
> Thank the LORD with harps, and sing praises to Him on the psaltery with ten strings.
This large psaltery with ten strings was used both in the morning and evening divine services. Psalm 92, verses 2, 3, 4:
> It is a precious thing to thank the LORD, and to sing praises to Your name, O Most High; to proclaim Your grace in the morning, and Your grace in the night, upon the ten strings and the psaltery, with playing upon the harp.
Furthermore, the psaltery has a very charming and lovely sound...

->of Solomon’s Temple.<-

They provided the tone and, during festivals of celebration, encouraged the Israelites toward joy in GOD. Therefore, David says in the 81st Psalm, verses 2 and 3: Sing joyfully to GOD, who is our strength; shout for joy to the GOD of Jacob. Take up the psalms and bring the drums <note>original: "Paucken," referring to timbrels or hand-drums</note>, the lovely harps with psalteries. These psalteries, as well as the harps, were also used by some godless Jews at the time of the Prophet **Amos** for worldly, sinful pleasure, for which he reproaches them for such sins in chapter 6, verses 5 and 6: You play upon the psalter and invent songs for yourselves like **David**, and drink wine from bowls, and anoint yourselves with balsam, and do not concern yourselves at all with the ruin of **Joseph** <note>This refers to the "ruin of Joseph," a metaphor for the northern kingdom of Israel being in distress while the elite lived in luxury</note>. When the **Moabites** and **Ammonites** came in great numbers to fight against **Jehoshaphat**, the King of Judah, GOD arranged it so that the enemies themselves fell into disagreement, so that they killed and destroyed one another. When **Jehoshaphat** and his people had finished dividing the spoil, they gathered together in the **Valley of Praise** <note>original: "Lobethal"; known in Hebrew as Emek Berakah</note>, for there they praised the LORD, because the LORD had given them joy over their enemies; and they entered Jerusalem with psalteries, harps, and trumpets to the house of the LORD. 2 Chronicles 20:1, 23, 28.

128) **The Trumpets and Pipes.** The trumpets <term>trombones or shofars <note>The German "Posaunen" here refers specifically to instruments made of ram's horns, used for signaling rather than melodic accompaniment</note></term> were made from ram’s horns and gave a strong, bright sound. Therefore, while the psalms were being sung, these trumpets and the clarions <note>original: "Drommeten"</note> were never used because of their strong sound, so that every word of the singing could be better understood. In the **Year of Release** or **Jubilee** <note>original: "Hall-Jahr"</note>, the trumpets were specifically blown throughout the entire land of Israel, at which time everyone returned to their own property. Leviticus 25:9–10: Then you shall let the trumpet sound throughout all your land on the tenth day of the seventh month, even on the Day of Atonement; and you shall hallow the fiftieth year and call it a Year of Release in the land for all who dwell therein. These trumpets were used especially in war when one wanted to quickly assemble an army. When **Ehud**, whom GOD had raised up as a savior for the children of Israel, had killed the King of the Moabites, **Eglon**, he blew the trumpet on the mountains of **Ephraim**, and a large army of the children of Israel gathered to him, and they struck down about ten thousand of the Moabites at that time, all of them the best and most valiant men, so that not one escaped. Judges 3:15, 21, 29. Likewise, the trumpets were blown when they wanted to raise a battle cry and strike terror into the enemies. **Gideon** gave three hundred men empty jars with torches inside, and a trumpet in each man's hand.

# Model

When they, in the darkness of night, came unnoticed near the camp of the Midianites, they smashed the jars so that the torches could be seen, blew the trumpets <note>original: "Posaunen"; in this context, referring to the shofars or ram's horns used by Gideon's men</note>, and cried out: "The sword for the LORD and for Gideon!" As the Midianites woke from their sleep and saw so many burning torches in the hands of their enemies who were so close, and heard the sound of the trumpets and the terrifying battle cry, they were greatly frightened and became dismayed; and the LORD caused every man’s sword to be turned against his fellow. <term>Judges 7:16, 20, 23 <note>original: "Indic. 7," from the Latin "Judicum"</note></term>

A signal was also given by the sound of the trumpets when those pursuing the enemy should stop and return. When Abner, Saul’s general, wanted to make Saul’s son, Ish-bosheth, king against David (who had been anointed king over Israel at Hebron), a fierce battle arose. When Abner and his people were defeated, he called out to Joab: "Shall the sword devour forever? Do you not know that it will lead to more misery in the end? How long will it be before you tell the people to stop pursuing their brothers?" And Joab blew the trumpets, and all the people stood still, and they pursued Israel no more, nor did they fight any longer. <term>2 Samuel 2:12, 26, 28</term>

Rebels also made use of trumpets when they wanted to rouse others to insurrection against their rightful authority. <term>2 Samuel 20,</term> <margin>1, 2, 22</margin> Sheba, a notorious and wicked man, blew the trumpets and said: "We have no portion in David, nor any inheritance in the son of Jesse; every man to his tent, O Israel!" Then everyone in Israel deserted David and followed Sheba, the son of Bichri. However, they later cut off the rebel's head and threw it over the wall to Joab.

When there was concern and danger regarding an invasion by enemies, a signal was given to those who were scattered using trumpets to show where they should assemble. When the Jews were building the walls of Jerusalem after the Babylonian captivity, their enemies wanted to prevent them. But Nehemiah said to the nobles and officials and to the rest of the people: "The work is large and extensive, and we are scattered on the wall, far from one another. In whatever place you hear the sound of the trumpet, gather there to us. Our GOD will fight for us." <term>Nehemiah 4:19, 20</term>

The pipes were a type of instrument similar to our flutes; their sound and tone were not as strong as that of the trumpets <note>original: "Trommeten," likely referring to the straight metal trumpets</note>, and much softer and gentler than that of the trumpets <note>original: "Posaunen," here referring to the louder horns</note>. They were made of reed-cane, which had a thin outer layer, was almost without a core, and was hollow inside. These pipes—

129) **The Court of the Priests** <term>Ezrat Ha-Kohanim <note>original Hebrew: "עזרת הכהנים"</note></term>, which was eleven cubits wide and 135 cubits long. It lay toward the east, opposite the Altar of Burnt Offering. No Israelite was permitted to enter this Court of the Priests, not even the King himself, except when someone had a sacrifice to offer; in that case, he entered this court, laid his hands upon the sacrificial animal, remained standing by the altar, watched as it was sacrificed, and performed his prayer in the meantime. However, those offering sacrifices did not ascend from the Court of Israel into the Court of the Priests; rather, they entered the inner court through the Sacrifice Gate and thus came into the Court of the Priests. The priests were descended from the tribe of Levi, and within that tribe, from the house or lineage of Aaron. Whenever they attended to their official duties in the Temple, this Court of the Priests was their place of residence, and they were required to wear the holy garments, which were snow-white clothes. They were not permitted to go near any dead person nor touch one, except for very close relatives, such as father and mother, brother and sister, son and daughter. **Leviticus 21:1, 2.** As long as they were performing their service and attendance in the Temple, they were also forbidden from drinking wine. **Ezekiel 44:21.** *The priests shall drink no wine when they enter the inner court.* They received a portion of the sacrifices. **Leviticus 7:32.** *The right shoulder you shall give to the priest as a heave offering <note>a gift lifted up or "heaved" as a contribution to the priest</note> from your thank-offerings.* **Chapter 10:12.** *And Moses spoke to Aaron and to his remaining sons, Eleazar and Ithamar: Take the grain offering that remains from the offerings of the LORD, and eat it without leaven beside the altar.* The priests had no inheritance of land in the Promised Land, as...

pipes <note>original: "Pfeiffen"; likely referring to the *halil* or ancient flute</note> were used only twelve times a year in the Temple: twice during the slaughter of the Passover Lamb, once during the Passover Festival, once at the Feast of Pentecost, and on all eight days of the entire Feast of Tabernacles <note>also known as Sukkot</note>, during which the Great Hallel <note>a specific sequence of Psalms (113–118) used for praise</note> was always sung to the sound of these instruments. 129) **The Priest's Court** <term>Azrat HaKohanim <note>original Hebrew: עזרת הכהנים</note></term> which was eleven cubits wide and 135 cubits long. The priests were descended from the tribe of Levi, specifically from the house or lineage of Aaron.
