They were not permitted to go near any dead person, nor touch one, except for their very close relatives, such as father and mother, brother and sister, son and daughter. **Leviticus 21:1, 2.** Also, for as long as they had their service and attendance in the Temple, they were not permitted to drink any wine. **Ezekiel 44:21.** *The priests shall drink no wine when they are to go into the inner court.* They received a portion of the sacrifices. **Leviticus 7:32.** *The right shoulder shall they give to the priest as a heave offering from their peace offerings.* And in **Chapter 10:12**, Moses speaks with Aaron and with his remaining sons, Eleazar and Ithamar: *Take the grain offering that remains of the offerings of the LORD made by fire, and eat it without leaven beside the altar.* The priests had no inheritance of land in the Promised Land, as...

<meta>This page continues the description of the regulations and duties of the priesthood within the Temple complex, specifically detailing their inheritance, their requirements for purity, and their role in communal life.</meta>

# Model

130.) ...the other Israelites. **Deuteronomy 18:1, 2.** The priests and the Levites of the entire tribe of Levi shall have no portion nor inheritance with Israel; they shall eat the offerings of the LORD and His inheritance. Therefore, they shall have no inheritance among their brothers, for the LORD is their inheritance, as He has spoken to them. If a priest sinned, he was required to offer a sacrifice for himself just as other Israelites did. **Leviticus 4:3.** If a priest who is anointed should sin, so that he brings guilt upon the people, then he shall bring for the sin which he has committed a young bullock <note>original: "Farren"</note> without blemish to the LORD for a sin offering. King David divided the priests into twenty-four orders, according to how the lot fell for each of their families. **1 Chronicles 25:35.** <note>The original citation "25, 35" likely refers to the division of the Temple musicians, while the 24 orders of priests are detailed in 1 Chronicles 24.</note> He who was physically infirm <note>referring to physical disabilities or blemishes</note> was permitted to enjoy his portion of the showbread and the offerings, but was not allowed to offer upon the altar itself, nor to enter the Holy Place. **Leviticus 21:23.** He shall eat the bread of his God, both of the holy and of the most holy; yet he shall not come to the veil <note>original: "Fürhange"</note> nor approach the altar, because there is a blemish in him, that he may not profane my sanctuary. 

When the people of God were to go into battle in war, the priests first had to exhort them to bravery. **Deuteronomy 20:2.** When you come near to the battle, the priest shall step forward and speak to the people and say to them: "Israel, listen; you go today into battle against your enemies; let not your heart faint, do not fear and do not tremble, and do not be terrified of them; for the LORD your God goes with you, to fight for you against your enemies, to help you." 

Aaron and his sons were anointed upon entering their priesthood, and through this, all their descendants received the right to the priesthood. If someone was to be accepted as a priest, the Great <term>Sanhedrin <note>original: "Synedrium", the supreme judicial and religious council in ancient Israel</note></term> had to judge whether he was capable of the priesthood. When God allowed general punishments to fall upon the land, the priests had to lie on the ground in garments of repentance <note>sackcloth</note> between the Temple and the Altar, and call upon God with a piteous cry for help and for the averting of the punishments. **Joel 2:17.** Let the priests, the ministers of the LORD, weep between the porch <note>original: "Hallen"</note> and the altar and say: "LORD, spare your people and do not let your heritage be put to shame." 

The priests were a type <note>a symbolic foreshadowing or "Fürbild"</note> both of Christ—who would make the cleansing of our sins through Himself—and also of those Christians who serve God, keep well the temple of their hearts, pray for others, and should bring their body and soul to God as a living, holy, and well-pleasing sacrifice. For Christ has made us to be kings and priests before God and His Father. **Revelation 1:6.** <note>original: "Apocal. 1, 6"</note>

<meta>This page continues the detailed description of the components and surroundings of the Altar of Burnt Offering within the Temple of Solomon.</meta>

### of Solomon’s Temple.

130) The two tables by the Altar of Burnt Offering. One was made of silver, the **Table of Silver** <note>original Hebrew: שֻׁלְחַן כֶּסֶף, *shulchan kesef*</note>, upon which the 93 golden vessels used in the daily divine service were placed.

131) The other table was made of marble, the **Table of the Fats** <note>original Hebrew: שֻׁלְחַן הַחֲלָבִים, *shulchan ha-chalavim*</note>, upon which the pieces of the sacrifice and their fat were placed before being carried up to the Altar of Burnt Offering.

132) The **Ash Pit** <note>original Hebrew: בֵּית הַדֶּשֶׁן, *beit ha-deshen*, literally 'House of Ashes'</note>, into which the ashes of the sacrificial animals—which had been slaughtered and burned upon the altar—were poured. These were later taken to a clean place outside the city of Jerusalem. This was to signify that after His death, Christ’s body should be laid in a clean, new tomb in which no dead person had ever been placed (John 19:41). The ashes on the altar were swept together and gathered into a heap from the three fires of the Altar of Burnt Offering; from there, they were carried down by the priests into this ash pit. Leviticus 6:9, 10, 11: *The burnt offering shall burn upon the altar all night until the morning. And the priest shall put on his linen robe, and his linen undergarments upon his body, and take up the ashes which the fire hath consumed with the burnt offering on the altar, and he shall pour them beside the altar. And he shall then put off his garments, and put on other garments, and carry the ashes forth outside the camp unto a clean place.*

133) The **Marble Slab** <note>original Hebrew: טַבְלָא שֶׁל שַׁיִשׁ, *tabla shel shayish*</note> which was placed over this pit; it had a ring <term>Rincken: an archaic term for a pull-ring or handle</term> by which it could be lifted off and replaced.

134) An **Ash Pot** <note>original Hebrew: סִיר דֶּשֶׁן, *sir deshen*</note>, with which the ashes were carried down from the Altar of Burnt Offering and poured into the ash pit.

135) The **Cavity** <note>original Hebrew: מְחִלָּה שִׁית, *mechila shit*; 'Shit' refers to the foundation drains or pits beneath the altar</note> by the Altar of Burnt Offering, into which the water—which flowed here from the spring of Etham—fell down.

136) The **Altar of Burnt Offering** <note>original Hebrew: מִזְבַּח הָעוֹלָה, *mizbach ha-olah*</note>, which did not stand directly opposite the door of the Temple, but was positioned slightly further along, from the North <note>original: "Mitternacht", literally midnight, an archaic German term for North</note> toward the South <note>original: "Mittag", literally midday, an archaic German term for South</note>. Because the spot where the Altar of Burnt Offering stood was the actual site on Mount Moriah where Abraham intended to sacrifice his son, and where David saw the Angel of the Lord, every other part of the entire construction had to be aligned according to this Altar of Burnt Offering and its specific location. For this reason, the entire inner and outer courtyards with their buildings were not situated in the center of Mount Moriah, but rather more toward the North...

# Model

...had come to stand. The Altar of Burnt Offering was made of unhewn stones which no iron tool had touched. Exodus 20:25: **If you will make me an altar of stone, you shall not build it of hewn stones.** The Jews give this reason: because iron shortens human life <note>original: "weil das Eisen das menschliche Leben verkürze," referring to iron as a material for weapons of war</note>, while the altar is intended to lengthen it; therefore, no iron was to be used in the construction of the altar. During the Festival of Booths <note>original: "Lauber-Hütten-Fest," also known as Sukkot</note>, green boughs <note>original: "Mayen," festive greenery or branches</note> were placed around the Altar of Burnt Offering in such a way that the branches bent over and touched the hearth of the altar. The words of Psalm 118:27 refer to this: **Adorn the feast with boughs, even unto the horns of the altar.** During the same festival, the Israelite people went around the altar once on each of the seven days (for that is how long it was celebrated) and sang with joy the words from that same Psalm, verse 25: **O LORD, help! O LORD, grant us success!** And on the seventh day, which was the holiest, they went around the altar seven times.

137) The triple ascent to the altar, <term>כֶּבֶשׁ מַעֲלֵה הַמִּזְבֵּחַ</term> <note>Hebrew: *kebesh ma'aleh ha-mizbeach*, meaning the ramp or walkway of the altar</note>, not via steps, but slanting upwards <note>original: "schraat," an archaic term for inclined or sloping</note>. Exodus 20:26.

138) The middle ascent, 16 cubits wide, 9 cubits high, and 32 cubits long, upon which the priests went up with the sacrificial pieces.

139) The two side ascents, 7 cubits wide, 6 cubits high, and 31 cubits long. One facing the East <note>original: "gegen Morgen," literally 'towards the morning'</note>, which the priests used to go up after circling the altar.

140) The other side ascent facing the West <note>original: "gegen Abend," literally 'towards the evening'</note>, by which the priests always descended again from the circuit of the altar.

141) The space between the southern buildings <note>original: "Mittags-Gebäuden," literally 'midday buildings'</note> and the foot of the altar.

142) The cavity on the middle ascent of the Altar of Burnt Offering, <term>רְבִיבָה</term> <note>Hebrew: *rebibah*, a small hollow or pit</note>, into which those birds were thrown that were found unfit for sacrifice.

143) The container in which the salt was kept, <term>מֶלַח לַאֲבָרִים</term> <note>Hebrew: *melach la-abarim*, salt for the sacrificial limbs</note>, with which all sacrifices had to be salted before they were placed upon the fire. For all sacrifices had to be salted first. Mark 9:49. This was to indicate that the covenant with God should be incorruptible, constant, and eternal. *You shall salt all your grain offerings, and your grain offering shall never be without the salt of the covenant of your God; for with all your offerings you shall offer salt.* <note>Reference to Leviticus 2:13</note>. Therefore, the covenant with God is called a "Covenant of Salt." Numbers 18:19. **It shall be an incorruptible covenant before the LORD** <term>בְּרִית מֶלַח</term> <note>Hebrew: *berit melach*, a covenant of salt</note> *Pactum salis seculi ad facies Do-* <note>Latin: "An everlasting covenant of salt before the Lo-", continuing the verse from Numbers 18:19</note>

It also details the specific dimensions and functions of the ramps used by priests to access the altar.</summary>

->### of Solomon's Temple.<-

...of the Lord <note>original: "mini," the conclusion of the Latin "Domini" from the previous page</note>; for the covenant established with GOD is steadfast and never wavers on God’s side. Isaiah 54:10: **Though the mountains may depart and the hills be removed, my grace shall not depart from you, and the covenant of my peace shall not be removed, says the LORD who has compassion on you.**

144) The place opposite the Temple where the priests remained standing and turned toward the Temple when they carried the heave offering or wave offering <note>referring to specific priestly movements where the sacrifice was lifted or swung as a gesture of dedication</note> in their hands toward the altar.

145) The base or the foundation of the altar <term>יְסוֹד הַמִּזְבֵּחַ</term> <note>Hebrew: *yesod ha-mizbeach*</note>; one cubit high, and 28 cubits long and wide.

146) The two tubes at the south and west corners of the Altar of Burnt Offering <term>נִקְבֵי הַיְסוֹד</term> <note>Hebrew: *nikvei ha-yesod*, "holes of the foundation"</note> <note>Latin: *Nares fundamenti*, "nostrils of the foundation"</note>, into which the remaining blood—which had to be poured out at the base of the altar—was emptied. From there, it flowed with the water from the Ethan spring into the Brook Kidron toward the Mount of Olives. In this way, all the sacrificial blood of the entire Old Testament marked that place where the final, most intense suffering of Christ—the great atoning sacrifice of the world—took its sorrowful beginning <note>referring to the Garden of Gethsemane, located near the Brook Kidron</note>, which then continued until his death. All remaining blood had to be poured at the base of the altar because, in the future, all the blood of Christ during his suffering was to be poured out upon the earth. And since at his death a few drops of blood still remained in his heart, his side and heart had to be opened with a spear so that all of Christ’s blood, down to the very last drop, would be shed, just as in the prefiguration <note>original: "Fürbilde," a historical event acting as a symbolic prophecy</note> all remaining blood had to be poured at the base of the altar.

147) The corner of the Altar of Burnt Offering located toward the east and south, which was removed <note>i.e., the corner was not built or was indented</note> because the altar was not intended to lie in two tribes, but in one—specifically in the tribe of Benjamin. Yet the boundary line from the tribe of Judah, which distinguished the two tribes, passed through that area and touched the altar exactly at this corner. To this, they apply the words of Moses’ blessing over the tribe of Benjamin: **He shall dwell between his shoulders.** Deuteronomy 33:12. Others believe that on the east and south sides, the corner was missing by one cubit.

148) The silver censer <note>original: "Räucher-Faß"</note> by the altar on the west side, from which a priest would take glowing coals from the Altar of Burnt Offering, pour them into a golden censer, and carry them into the Holy Place to perform the incense offering.

# Model

149) The **Scarlet Thread** <note>original: "Filum miniatum"</note>, the red stripe or line that ran around the middle of the Altar of Burnt Offering <term>חוט הסיקרא</term> <note>Hebrew: *Chut ha-Sikra*</note>. Above this line, the blood from the burnt offerings of doves and turtledoves was applied; below the line, the blood of animals and the sin offerings of birds was placed. This blood served, as it were, as the ransom for sin. Leviticus 17:11: **For the blood is the atonement for the life.** This was a precursor to the great, universal atonement that was to occur through the blood of Christ, of which the Holy Apostle Peter says in 1 Peter 1:18–19: **Know that you were not redeemed with corruptible things, like silver or gold, from your aimless conduct received by tradition from your fathers, but with the precious blood of Christ, as of a lamb without blemish and without spot.** Through faith in the holy blood of JESUS, which encompasses his entire merit, a person becomes clean from their sins and attains full forgiveness. For **the blood of JESUS Christ, the Son of God, cleanses us from all sin.** 1 John 1:7. Therefore, it is said of the chosen ones, who have been made perfectly righteous and purified of all their sins through Christ: **They have washed their robes and made them white in the blood of the Lamb.** Revelation 7:14. Thus, all sacrifice and blood-shedding in the Old Testament was a very clear foreshadowing of the future bloody suffering and death of JESUS Christ.

150) The **walkway around the altar** <term>סובב מזבח</term> <note>Hebrew: *Sovev Mizbeach*</note> where the priest walked when he had to sprinkle the blood onto the horns of the altar, dipping his finger into it and then letting the blood drip onto the horns of the altar.

151) The other, higher walkway around the hearth of the altar, one cubit wide <term>מקום הילוך רגלי הכהנים</term> <note>Hebrew: *Makom Hiluch Raglei Ha-Kohanim*</note> <note>Latin: *Locus ambulationis pedum sacerdotum*, meaning "the place for the walking of the priests' feet"</note>.

152) The rim around this higher walkway, one cubit wide.

153) The **four horns of the Altar of Burnt Offering** <term>קרנות המזבח</term> <note>Hebrew: *Karnot Ha-Mizbeach*</note> in its four corners, which were small, square, protruding pillars, one cubit high and wide.

154) The **hearth of the Altar of Burnt Offering** <term>מקום המערכה</term> <note>Hebrew: *Makom Ha-Ma'arachah*</note> <note>Latin: *Locus struis*, meaning "the place of the woodpile"</note>, 20 cubits long and wide.

155) The four corners of the hearth, which were cut away so that the priests could walk around the altar more easily.

156) The **two silver vessels** on the altar <term>שני ספלים של כסף</term> <note>Hebrew: *Sheni Sefalim shel Kesef*</note> <note>Latin: *duo simpula seu vasa argentea*</note>, one of which stood toward the East, into which the wine was poured that was used for the daily drink offering <note>The text cuts off here at the word "ges," likely for "gegossen" (poured)</note>

->of the Temple of Solomon.<-

was sacrificed. With every <term>grain offering<note>original German: "Speis-Opfer"; a sacrifice consisting of flour, oil, and frankincense.</note></term> there was usually also a <term>drink offering<note>original German: "Tranck-Opfer"; a libation or liquid sacrifice.</note></term>. When a lamb was sacrificed, about half a pot of wine was offered; for an ox or a bull, a whole pot of wine was offered—calculating by our own measures—and poured into this silver vessel upon the altar. Through an internal pipe, the wine fell down into the earth and was conducted through an underground passage, along with the water from the Etham spring, into the Brook Kidron. Salt was also thrown into the wine, because all sacrifices were required to be salted. This drink offering was the final act of every burnt offering; during its pouring, the priests blew trumpets and the Levites sang the prescribed Psalms.

157) The other silver vessel, which stood on the altar toward the **West** <note>original: "Abend" (evening), a traditional German term for the West.</note>, was where water mixed with wine was poured and offered to God during the **Feast of Tabernacles** <note>The Jewish festival of Sukkot.</note>. This was done out of gratitude toward God, who had long ago quenched the thirst of their ancestors in the wilderness—during their great need and total lack of water—from the dry rock which Moses had to strike with his staff. *Exodus 17:6.* Furthermore, this was a <term>prefiguration<note>A symbolic "foreshadowing" of later religious events.</note></term> of the future pouring out of the Holy Spirit, which was to happen in abundant fullness in the New Testament. *John 7:37, 38, 39.* That this ceremony was intended for this purpose is evident from the words of Christ, who was present in the Temple on the seventh day of the Feast of Tabernacles. He watched the pouring of the water and explained it to all who were present. **On the last day of the feast, which was the most magnificent, Jesus stood up, cried out and said: If anyone thirsts, let him come to me and drink. He who believes in me, as the Scripture says, from his body shall flow streams of living water. But he said this of the Spirit, which those who believed in him were to receive.** *John 7:37, 38, 39.* The water was fetched from the Well of Siloam, which was outside the city of Jerusalem. When the priest who carried it entered the inner court through the Water Gate, everyone in the Temple sang and the Levites made music: **With joy you shall draw water from the wells of salvation.** *Isaiah 12:3.*

158) The three fires that burned constantly on the altar. The first fire was toward the **North** <note>original: "Mitternacht" (midnight), a traditional German term for the North.</note>, which was called the **eternal fire** <note>original Hebrew: מַעֲרָכָה שֶׁל אֵשׁ תָּמִיד (ma'arakhah shel esh tamid), meaning "the arrangement of the continual fire."</note>. It had first been ignited from heaven at the dedication of the Temple. *2 Chronicles 7:1.* It is counted among the won—

...those who were constantly present at the Temple recounted that the fire on the altar could never be quenched or extinguished by any downpour of rain, and that the smoke from it always rose upward as a straight pillar and could be neither blown nor moved to the right or left by any storm wind.

159) The second fire toward the Morning <note>East</note>, upon which the pieces of the sacrifice were placed and burned: <term>the arrangement for sacrifices<note>original Hebrew: מַעֲרָכָה שֶׁל זְבָחִים (ma'arakhah shel zevachim)</note></term>.

160) The third fire on the West <note>original: "Abend-Seite" (Evening-side)</note> side, <term>the arrangement for incense<note>original Hebrew: מַעֲרֶכֶת הַקְּטֹרֶת (ma'arekhet ha-ketoret)</note></term>, about four cubits across toward the North; from this fire the glowing coals were taken, with which the lamps on the golden lampstands were lit and the incense was burned on the Altar of Incense in the Holy Place.

161) **The Lot**—not far from the Altar of Burnt Offering—which lay in a box and consisted of two metal plates. Upon one of these was written in Hebrew words, **"for the Lord,"** and on the other, **"for Azazel,"** or rather **"toward Azazel,"** which indicated a place in the wilderness where the <term>scapegoat<note>original German: "Versühnbock" (atonement goat)</note></term> was led and cast down from the rocks. On the **Day of Atonement** <note>Yom Kippur</note>, two goats were presented, and the lot was to fall upon them to determine which should be sacrificed to the Lord and which, as a scapegoat, should carry the sins of the people into the wilderness. While the two goats stood beside the High Priest, he grasped the metal plates and placed one on each goat. The goat upon which the plate with the word **"for the Lord"** fell was sacrificed; the one upon which the plate with the word **"toward Azazel"** was placed was sent into the wilderness. Along the way, however, it was cursed and spat upon by all the people, which was a <term>prefiguration<note>A symbolic foreshadowing of a future religious event.</note></term> of Christ. For "Christ has redeemed us from the curse of the law, having become a curse for us." *Galatians 3:13.*

162) **The two low pillars**, upon which two priests stood with trumpets during the <term>drink offering<note>original German: "Tranck-Opfer"; a liquid sacrifice of wine.</note></term> and joined in with the singing and music of the Levites. The trumpets were made of silver, almost a cubit long, and no Levite was permitted to carry them; they were reserved only for the priests who were of the lineage of Aaron. These were used seven times every day in the Temple: once in the morning when the Temple gates were to be opened, then three times during the morning service, and three times during the evening service. Never fewer than two and never more than one hundred and twenty were used at once.

163) **The Place of Slaughter** <term>the slaughtering house<note>original Hebrew: בֵּית הַמִּטְבָּחַיִם (beit ha-mitbachayim)</note></term>, eight cubits from the Burnt Offering Al-

->### of the Temple of Solomon.<-

...altar of sacrifice, where the most holy offerings were slaughtered toward the North <note>The author uses "Mitternacht" (midnight) to denote the North, a common convention in early modern German orientation.</note>.

164) The 24 iron half-rings <term>rings <note>original Hebrew: טַבָּעוֹת (tabba'ot)</note></term>, one end of which was fixed into the ground, while the other end could also be secured. This allowed a single person to manage the killing of a sacrificial animal alone, once the ring was struck around its neck and it was trapped within. This served as a prefiguration of the capture of **Jesus Christ**, through which he has set us into the freedom of the children of **God**. Galatians 5:1.

165) The 8 marble slaughter-tables <term>tables of marble <note>original Hebrew: שֻׁלְחָנוֹת שֶׁל שַׁיִשׁ (shulchanot shel shayish)</note></term>, always arranged in pairs, upon which the sacrificial pieces were divided into smaller portions. When an Israelite, during the carving of the sacrificial meat, saw before his eyes the backbone and the internal parts—the heart, lungs, and liver—he was to reflect that **God** likewise sees into your innermost self. To this the Holy Apostle Paul directs his aim when he speaks in the Epistle to the Hebrews: "All things are naked and opened before his eyes." <note>original Greek: Πάντα δὲ γυμνὰ καὶ τετραχηλισμένα τοῖς ὀφθαλμοῖς αὐτοῦ (Panta de gymna kai tetrachēlismena tois ophthalmois autou)</note>. 167) The cedar beams, which stood upright within these low marble pillars and had three iron hooks on each side to conveniently hang and slaughter larger and smaller sacrificial animals.

168) The space where these pillars stood, which was thirteen and a half cubits wide.

170) The remaining five bronze basins on the North side, in which the sacrificial pieces were washed in the cleanest and purest manner before they were placed upon the Altar of Burnt Offering.

of those <note>This refers to the miracles associated with the Temple service.</note> who were constantly found at the Temple, it is recounted that the fire on the Altar could never be extinguished nor put out by any downpour of rain, and that the smoke from it always rose into the heights as a straight column, and could be moved or blown neither to the right nor to the left by any storm-wind. <note>This "straight column of smoke" is one of the ten miracles of the Temple recorded in the Mishnah (Pirkei Avot 5:5).</note>

On one was written in Hebrew words, *For the Lord*, and on the other, *For Azazel*, or rather *Toward Azazel*. This indicated a place in the wilderness where the scapegoat was led and cast down from a cliff. On the Day of Atonement, two goats were presented, and the lot was to fall upon them to determine which would be sacrificed to the Lord and which, as a scapegoat, would carry the sins of the people into the wilderness. While the two goats stood beside the High Priest, he drew the plates and placed one on each goat. The goat upon which the plate with the words *For the Lord* fell was sacrificed; the one upon which the plate with the words *Toward Azazel* was placed was sent into the wilderness. Along the way, it was cursed and spat upon by the people, which served as a prefiguration of **Christ**. For **Christ** has redeemed us from the curse of the law by becoming a curse for us. Galatians 3:13. <note>original Latin: "Christus nos redemit de maledicto legis, factus pro nobis maledictum."</note>

162) The two low pillars upon which two priests stood with trumpets during the drink offering <note>The libation of wine that accompanied many sacrifices.</note>, joining in the singing and music of the Levites. The trumpets were made of silver, nearly a cubit long; no Levite was permitted to carry them, only the priests of the lineage of Aaron. These were used in the Temple seven times each day: once in the morning when the Temple gates were opened, then three times during the morning service, and three times during the evening service. Never fewer than two and never more than one hundred and twenty trumpets were used at once.

163) The Place of Slaughter <term>house of slaughtering <note>original Hebrew: בֵּית הַמִּטְבָּחִים (Beit ha-Mitbachim)</note></term>, 8 cubits from the Burnt Offer-

### of Solomon’s Temple.

Altar of Sacrifice, where the most holy offerings were slaughtered toward the North <note>original: "Mitternacht" (Midnight), which in historical German orientation refers to the North.</note>.

164) The 24 iron half-rings <term>rings <note>original Hebrew: טַבָּעוֹת (tabba'ot)</note></term>, which were fixed into the ground at one end, and could also be fastened at the other, so that a single person could manage the killing of a sacrificial animal when the ring was struck around its neck and it was trapped therein. This was a prefiguration of the taking captive of **Jesus** Christ, through which He has placed us in the liberty of the children of **God**. Galatians 5:1.

165) The 8 marble slaughter tables <term>tables of marble <note>original Hebrew: שֻׁלְחָנוֹת שֶׁל שַׁיִשׁ (shulchanot shel shayish)</note></term>, always two next to one another, upon which the sacrificial pieces were divided into smaller portions. When an Israelite, during such a division of the sacrificial pieces, saw the backbone and then the internal parts—heart, lungs, and liver—exposed before his eyes, he was meant to remember: even so does **God** see your inward parts. To this the Holy Apostle Paul directs his attention when he speaks in the Epistle to the Hebrews: All things are naked and opened unto His eyes. <note>original Greek: Πάντα δὲ γυμνὰ καὶ τετραχηλισμένα τοῖς ὀφθאלμοῖς αὐτοῦ (Panta de gymna kai tetrachelismena tois ophthalmois autou)</note>. 167) The cedar beams, which stood upright in these low marble columns and had 3 iron hooks on each side, to conveniently hang and slaughter larger and smaller sacrificial animals upon them.

168) The space where these columns stood, which was twelve and a half cubits wide.

171) The bronze stands <term>bases of bronze <note>original Hebrew: מְכוֹנוֹת נְחֹשֶׁת (mechonot nechoshet)</note></term>, upon which the basins stood. Each was 4 cubits long and wide, and 3 cubits high. 1 Kings 7:27.

# Model

from one rim to the other, and 30 cubits in circumference. When it was completely full, it held 3,000 baths <note>A "bath" is an ancient Hebrew liquid measure, roughly 22 to 40 liters.</note>, and when it was not quite full, it held 2,000 baths. Its rim was curved outwards like a bell, or like opening roses and lilies. 1 Kings 7:23, 24, 26. From the water of this bronze sea, the priests had to wash their hands and feet; and before this was done, no one was permitted to perform any duty of the priestly office. The water was led into it from the spring of Etham through hidden channels beneath the ground. The bronze sea was a prototype <term>Fürbild: a prophetic symbol or "type" in theology where an Old Testament object foreshadows a New Testament reality</term> of Holy Baptism; for just as no priest could approach the altar who had not first bathed with the water from the bronze sea, so in the New Testament, no spiritual priest—which all Christians are meant to be (Revelation 1:6)—should approach the Altar and Holy Communion who has not first been cleansed of sins through Holy Baptism.

# of Solomon’s Temple.

193) The golden door of the Temple <note>original Hebrew: שער האיתון, *Sha'ar ha-Aitun*, often associated with the Entrance Gate</note> where the door-wings, posts, and thresholds were overlaid with gold. It is said that at the Festival of Atonement, a red ribbon was formerly hung above this door; if God had been gracious to the people, it would turn snow-white. This is likely what Isaiah is referring to in these words: **"Though your sins are like scarlet, they shall be as white as snow; though they are red like crimson, they shall be as wool."** Isaiah 1:18.

194) The tower of the Temple, 120 cubits <note>A cubit is a biblical unit of measurement, approximately 18 inches or 45 centimeters.</note> high.

195) The golden roof of the same <note>original Hebrew: גג הבית, *Gag ha-Bayit*, meaning "the roof of the House"</note> which rose steeply upward and had railings around it. Very early in the morning, those priests who had been chosen by lot were required to climb up there and keep watch until it became bright enough that they could see the city of Hebron, which was not too far away; they then had to report this from above. Thereupon the Captain of the Temple, who was already present below, immediately made arrangements for the divine service and called out with a loud voice: **"You priests, come to the altar! You Levites, come to your pulpit! You Israelites, come to your stations!"** At this, everyone in the Temple began to stir, and the start of the divine service was made. This highest place of the entire Temple is without doubt the "pinnacle of the temple" <note>original Greek: τὸ πτερύγιον τοῦ ἱεροῦ, *to pterygion tou hierou*</note>, where Satan placed Christ during the temptation. Matthew 4:5.

196) The fourfold pillars, which stood one above the other at the porch of the Temple.

197) The four rooms in the uppermost story.

198) The two crowns that stood in the two uppermost windows of the Temple, one made of gold and the other of silver. By God's command, these were placed upon the High Priest Joshua by the prophet Zechariah, and were afterwards kept in the Temple as a memorial before the LORD. Zechariah 6:11.

199) The porch of the Temple <note>original Hebrew: אולם הבית, *Ulam ha-Bayit*</note> <note>original Latin: *propylæum templi*, meaning the entrance or vestibule</note>, 10 cubits long from east <note>original: "Morgen," meaning morning or East</note> to west <note>original: "Abend," meaning evening or West</note>, 20 cubits wide from south <note>original: "Mittag," meaning midday or South</note> to north <note>original: "Mitternacht," meaning midnight or North</note>, and 90 cubits high up to the upper chambers; all walls around were overlaid with gold.

200) The winding staircase <note>original Hebrew: לולים, *Lulim*</note> which stood at the western wall in the porch, by which one could climb up to the roof of the Temple.

201) The golden chains <note>original Hebrew: שרשרות זהב, *Sarsarot zahav*</note> which were kept in the porch of the Temple and were so large that on the links—

# Model

...the backs of the same, as if on steps, one was able to climb up into the uppermost rooms of the Temple. <note>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing from the previous page's description of the Temple's roof structure.</note>

202) The great golden lamps, which were donated to the Temple by Queen Helena, who had left paganism and converted to the true God of Israel. <note>Helena of Adiabene was a 1st-century queen who converted to Judaism and was known for her generosity to the Temple in Jerusalem.</note>

203) The golden vine <note>Hebrew: גֶּפֶן זָהָב, *gefen zahav*</note>, on which the branches and leaves were made of pure gold, but the berries of the clusters were made of precious gemstones. Whenever an Israelite found precious and rare gemstones among his possessions, he dedicated them to the Temple, so that these treasures eventually became priceless.

204) The containers and cupboards in the porch of the Temple, in which were kept the many golden vessels that Solomon had made—not so much for daily use, but for the glory of the House of the LORD.

205) The two tables in the porch of the Temple <note>Hebrew: שְׁנֵי שֻׁלְחָנוֹת בָּאוּלָם, *shenei shulchanot ba-ulam*</note>: the first was a marble table to the right of the gate that led into the Holy Place, toward the south, upon which the Showbread was placed before it was carried into the Holy Place. <term>Showbread: twelve loaves of bread set out weekly as an offering to God.</term>

206) The other table was of gold, to the left of the gate, toward the north, upon which the Showbread of the previous week was placed when it was carried out of the Holy Place. There, the High Priest ate his portion of it, for they had to be eaten in a holy place.

207) The gate from the porch into the Holy Place: 10 cubits wide, 20 cubits high. <note>A cubit is roughly 18 inches.</note>

[aussät]zig <note>original: "zig", completing the word "aussätzig" (leprous) from the previous page</note> until his death. 2 Chronicles 26:16–21. When the priest lit the incense upon the altar, the people prayed outside in the meantime. David refers to this in the words: *Let my prayer be set before you as incense, and the lifting up of my hands as the evening sacrifice.* Psalm 141:2 <note>source cites Psalm 145:2</note>. Primarily, however, this foreshadowed the prayer and intercession of Jesus Christ, who entered the Holy Place in heaven to appear before the face of God for us. Hebrews 9:24. It was at this Altar of Incense that Zacharias saw the angel of the Lord and, because he did not believe his words, was struck mute. Luke 1:11, 20.

215) The four horns of the Altar of Incense, which stood upright in its four corners like small square pillars.

216) The crown of gold that encircled the top of the altar.

217) The golden censer <term>incense burner</term> upon the altar, into which the holy incense was poured onto the glowing coals.

218) The eleven golden showbread tables <term>shulchanot lachmei ha-panim <note>Hebrew: "tables of the bread of the faces (Presence)"</note></term>, each one and a half cubits high. Solomon had ten made, and the eleventh was the one made by Moses for the Tabernacle. They stood on the north side, two and a half <note>original: "drittehalbe," an archaic German term for 2.5</note> cubits from the wall. Whenever two priests entered the Holy Place to remove the showbread from the previous week, two others were present at the same time to immediately place fresh showbread on the tables in their place, so that the tables were never empty for even a single moment. These loaves of showbread were a foreshadowing of Christ, who is the true Bread of Life that feeds and satisfies us unto eternal life. John 6:48.

219) The square marble stone on the north side <term>tabla shel parashat sotah <note>Hebrew: "tablet for the section on the suspected adulteress"</note>; <note>Latin: "tabula mulieris de adulterio suspectæ" — table of the woman suspected of adultery</note></term>, one cubit long and wide, with a golden ring by which it could be lifted. Beneath it was a pit from which dust was taken to be thrown into the "cursed bitter water" used in the ritual for a wife suspected of adultery. Numbers 5:17.

220) The two low golden pillars <term>shenei kannei ha-zahav <note>Hebrew: "two bases of gold"</note></term> located not far from the entrance to the Most Holy Place. On the Day of Atonement <term>Yom Kippur</term>, the High Priest would place the golden basins upon these pillars when he emerged from the Most Holy Place after sprinkling the sacrificial blood seven times toward the Mercy Seat.

### of Solomon’s Temple.

<margin>The dividing wall <note>original: Kotel ha-Mavdil</note></margin>
221) The wall between the Holy Place and the Most Holy Place, 20 cubits high and one cubit thick. <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length based on the distance from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger, approximately 18 inches.</note>

222) The golden netting and lattice-work above this wall, 10 cubits high, through which the smoke rising from the Altar of Incense could penetrate into the Most Holy Place.

223) The gate to the Most Holy Place <term>The Gate of the Sanctuary <note>original: Sha'ar ha-Devir</note></term> which was pentagonal or five-sided. The width of the gate was 7 cubits; the height of its posts was 6 cubits up to the beginning of the angle, which added a significant height.

224) The two door-leaves of the gate, which always stood open and were opened outward toward the Holy Place, never being locked.

225) The curtain before the Most Holy Place <term>The Veil <note>original: Parokhet</note></term> artistically interwoven with Cherubim and floral patterns, so that no one could see in from the outside. In the second Temple, instead of the wall that was between the Holy Place and the Most Holy Place, there were two curtains extending from the south to the north wall. And these very curtains are those which, at the suffering of Christ, were torn from top to bottom. Matthew 27:51.

226) The Most Holy Place <term>The Holy of Holies <note>original: Kodesh ha-Kodashim</note></term>, 20 cubits long, 20 cubits wide, and 30 cubits high. No one was permitted to enter it except the High Priest on only one day of the year—on the Day of Atonement—and then in snow-white garments. This represented the perfect purity and holiness of *Jesus Christ*, the one true High Priest. Hebrews 7:26–27. On the Day of Atonement, the High Priest entered the Most Holy Place four times: first with glowing coals and incense; the second time with the blood of the bull; the third time with the blood of the goat that was sacrificed to the Lord; and the fourth time he went in to retrieve the incense burner, which he had left standing in the Most Holy Place throughout the day. He did not remain there long but offered a short prayer and sprinkled the blood—once upward and seven times toward the ground against the Mercy Seat. At that moment, the glory of God revealed itself in the pillar of cloud through a powerful, fiery radiance. When he entered the Most Holy Place, no one was present, neither in the Holy Place nor in the forecourt or hall of the Temple. As soon as his official duties were completed there, he immediately came out, for he was filled with a holy fear of God, and the people outside also stood in anxiety on his account, because some High [Priests]...

# Model

h-Priests, who had not observed God’s command in all things, had been killed by God. <note>This continues from the previous page, referring to the high standards of holiness required to enter the Most Holy Place.</note>

227) The floor in the Most Holy Place was covered with thick golden plates.

228) The walls in the Most Holy Place were overlaid with gold all around and adorned with palm trees and Cherubim, and also set with precious gemstones.

229) The windows in the Most Holy Place—which was the most eminent part of the house—<term>Windows <note>original Hebrew: חלוֹנוֹת, challonot</note></term> 1 Kings 6:4.

230) The Ark of the Covenant <term>The Ark of the Testimony <note>original Hebrew: אֲרוֹן הָעֵדֻת, Aron ha-Edut</note></term> was overlaid with gold on both the outside and the inside. Moses had it constructed at two and a half cubits long, and one and a half cubits wide and high. Inside it lay the two stone Tablets of the Law side by side, which were one cubit long and wide, half a cubit thick, and were inscribed on both sides. Exodus 25:10. Likewise, the golden jar with Manna was kept there <term>The Jar of Manna <note>original Hebrew: צִנְצֶנֶת הַמָּן, tzintzenet ha-man</note></term> which, through a perpetual miracle, was preserved here for over 400 years without spoiling, whereas it would normally spoil by the very next day, except on the Sabbath. Exodus 16:14, 20, 27. Similarly, the blooming Rod of Aaron was preserved there <term>Aaron’s Staff <note>original Hebrew: מַטֵּה אַהֲרֹן, matteh Aharon</note></term> which bore blossoms and ripe almonds, and also had green shoots and leaves. Numbers 17:8. Whenever the High Priest asked God for counsel, he stood in the Holy Place and always turned himself toward the Ark of the Covenant. It was placed in the Most Holy Place exactly as it had been previously in the Tabernacle, so that its carrying poles extended eastward toward the Holy Place, and the length of the Ark stood according to the width of the Temple. When it was to be carried forward by the Levites during the journeys in the wilderness, it first had to be veiled and wrapped by the priests in the curtain that hung between the Holy Place and the Most Holy Place. Numbers 4:5. During the travels of the children of Israel in the wilderness, it was always carried at the head of the entire army. When it was lifted up, Moses prayed:

### of the Temple of Solomon.

...the Ark of the **Lord** was carried around for seven days in succession, and on the seventh day it was carried around seven times, at which point the walls of the city fell down and the children of Israel conquered it. Joshua 6:1–21. When the Philistines had put the Israelite people to flight and captured the Ark of the **Lord** as transitionary booty, they placed it in the temple of their idol, Dagon. When they rose early the next morning, they found Dagon lying on his face before the Ark of the **Lord**. After they set the idol back in its place, they found him the following morning lying on his face on the ground again, but his head and both his hands were cut off upon the threshold. 1 Samuel 4:11; 5:1–4. In the time of David, the Ark was in the City of David, where he had brought it from the house of Obed-Edom. 2 Samuel 6:12, 23. However, after Solomon built the Temple, the Ark of the **Lord**, along with the Tabernacle of the Congregation and all the holy vessels, was brought up from the City of David into the Temple, into the Most Holy Place under the wings of the Cherubim. 1 Kings 8:1–6.

238) **The Pillar of Cloud and Fire** / <note>original Hebrew: ענני כבוד יהוה (anane kavod YHWH); Latin: nubes gloriæ Domini</note> clouds of the glory of the **Lord**, in which the almighty **God** dwelt, and through such a visible sign made His presence known. This cloud stood upward like a pillar; on the outside was the cloud, and on the inside was the fire. In this Pillar of Cloud, **God** went before the people of Israel in the wilderness. Exodus 13:21–22. *The **Lord** went before them by day in a pillar of cloud to lead them on the right path, and by night in a pillar of fire to give them light, so they could travel day and night.* Often this cloud spread itself over the entire camp of the Israelites and served as a screen and a shadow for them. The Holy Apostle Paul refers to this in 1 Corinthians 10:1, saying: *Our fathers were all under the cloud.* When Pharaoh pursued the people of Israel with his army, **God** was their protection in the Pillar of Cloud and Fire, and from it, He let fiery rays fall upon the Egyptians. Exodus 14:24. *And it came to pass, that in the morning watch the **Lord** looked unto the host of the Egyptians through the pillar of fire and of the cloud, and troubled the host of the Egyptians.* This Pillar of Cloud and Fire was also seen on Mount Sinai. Exodus 24:16–18. *And the glory of the **Lord** abode upon Mount Sinai, and the cloud covered it six days: and the seventh day He called Moses out of the midst of the cloud. Leviticus 16:2. Whenever **God** was angered, the Pillar of Cloud turned away. 248) The Tabernacle of **Moses**, with all its accessories, which was brought into the Temple and placed in the Upper Chamber after the divine service within it had ceased. ***1 Kings 8:4.*** **GOD** himself was the architect of the Tabernacle; for He wanted a certain place to be designated for public worship among the people of **Israel**. Therefore, He gave the command to **Moses** for the **Israelites** in ***Exodus 25:8***: *They shall make me a sanctuary, that I may dwell among them.* **Moses** was not permitted to design the construction of the Tabernacle as he wished, but rather as **GOD** showed it to him in an image. ***Exodus 25:9.*** *According to all that I show thee, after the pattern of the dwelling, and the pattern of all its instruments, even so shall ye make it.* However, **GOD** had specifically chosen **Bezalel** for this task and given him wisdom and understanding to arrange the Tabernacle and all its accessories in an orderly and artistic manner. ***Exodus 31:1–5.*** *And the **LORD** spake unto **Moses**, saying: See, I have called by name **Bezalel**, the son of **Uri**, the son of **Hur**, of the tribe of **Judah**: And I have filled him with the spirit of **God**, in wisdom, and in understanding, and in knowledge, and in all manner of work, to work artistically in gold, silver, and brass, and in the artistic cutting and setting of stones, and in the artistic carving of wood, to make all manner of work.* **GOD** also gave him many other fellow workers to whom He likewise granted skill and wisdom for this purpose. ***Exodus 31:6.*** *And behold, I have given with him **Ahisamach** <note>In the biblical text of Exodus 31, the worker is Oholiab, the son of Ahisamach.</note> of the tribe of **Dan**, and in the hearts of all the wise I have put wisdom, that they may make all that I have commanded thee.* The required expenses for the construction of the Tabernacle were given voluntarily by the **Israelites**. ***Exodus 35:4, 5.*** *And **Moses** spake unto all the congregation of the children of **Israel**: This is the thing which the **LORD** commanded, saying: Take ye from among you a heave offering <term>Hebopfer: a type of voluntary contribution or "lifted" offering for the sanctuary</term> unto the **LORD**; <meta>This page continues the description of the Mosaic Tabernacle, focusing on the encampment of the tribes around it, the duties of the priests and Levites during its transport, its eventual placement in Solomon's Temple, and its symbolic significance as a "type" or prefiguration of Jesus Christ.</meta>

# Model
<meta>catchword: of God</meta>

Aaron and his sons shall wear the holy garments when they go into the Tabernacle of the Congregation <term>Stifts-Hütte|The portable sanctuary used by the Israelites during the Exodus; literally the "Tent of the Meeting."</term> or approach the altar to serve in the sanctuary, so that they do not bear their iniquity and die. In the wilderness, the twelve tribes always camped around this Tabernacle. *Numbers 2:2.* The children of Israel shall camp around the Tabernacle of the Congregation, each under his own banner and standard, according to their father's house. The priests always had their camp closest to the Tabernacle. *Numbers 3:38.* Before the dwelling and the Tabernacle of the Congregation toward the east <note>original: "gegen Morgen"; literally "towards the morning," referring to the sunrise in the east.</note> shall camp Moses, Aaron, and his sons, to keep charge of the sanctuary and the children of Israel. If a stranger approaches, he shall die. When the Tabernacle was dismantled for travel at the start of a journey, the priests had to wrap all the holy vessels in coverings, and only then were they taken up and carried by the Levites. *Numbers 4:15.* When Aaron and his sons have finished covering the sanctuary and all its furnishings when the camp sets out, then the children of Kohath shall go in to carry them; but they shall not touch the sanctuary, lest they die. After the people of Israel finally came to rest in the land of Canaan after their weary journey, and King Solomon later built the Temple, the Tabernacle with all its belongings was brought up into it. *1 Kings 8:2, 3, 4.* And all the men of Israel assembled before King Solomon in the month of Ethanim, at the feast, which is the seventh month. And when all the elders came, the priests lifted up the Ark of the Lord and brought it up, along with the Tabernacle of the Congregation and all the holy vessels that were in the Tabernacle. 

This Tabernacle was a prefiguration <term>Fürbild|A "type" or prophetic symbol in the Old Testament that points toward a reality in the New Testament.</term> of Christ. For the Word became flesh and dwelt <note>original: "ἐσκήνωσε" (eskēnōse); literally "pitched his tent" or "tabernacled," mirroring the language of the Tabernacle.</note> among us. *John 1:14.* The Tabernacle was in the midst of the camps of the children of Israel, and where believers are gathered in Christ’s name, there He is in the midst of them. *Matthew 18:20.* In the Most Holy Place of the Tabernacle, God dwelt, and in Christ, the entire fullness of the Godhead dwelt bodily. *Colossians 2:9.* Outwardly, the Tabernacle was very unsightly because of its rough coverings, yet inside everything shone with gold and many precious stones; and Christ, although He was in the form of God, did not consider it a thing to be grasped to be equal with God, but emptied Himself and took the form of a servant, became like any other man, and was found in human appearance and form. *Philippians 2:6, 7.* The Tabernacle was dismantled during travel, and then also after

L 3
<meta>catchword: with-</meta>

...would <note>original: "würden." This word completes a sentence likely describing the Levites' dedication to God's service from the previous page.</note>, therefore the Tribe of Levi <term>Tribe of Levi|One of the twelve tribes of Israel, chosen to serve in the Sanctuary rather than receiving a land inheritance.</term> was to have no portion of the Promised Land during its distribution; instead, God gave them the tithe in its place. Numbers 18:21.

> To the children of Levi I have given all the tithes in Israel as an inheritance for their service, which they perform for me at the Tabernacle of the Congregation.

The tithe had to be given to them not only from the grain of the field, but also from the orchard produce and the fruits of the gardens. Leviticus 27:30.

> All tithes in the land, whether of the seed of the land or of the fruit of the trees, belong to the Lord and shall be holy to the Lord.

The tithe also had to be given from the vineyards, olive groves, and honey production. 2 Chronicles 31:5.

> The children of Israel gave the firstfruits of grain, wine, oil, honey, and all kinds of produce from the field, and they brought in an abundance of every kind of tithe.

Likewise, the tithe was given from all livestock. Leviticus 27:32.

> And all tithes of cattle and sheep, and whatever passes under the rod <note>This refers to the practice of counting animals as they exited a pen; a rod dipped in colored ink would mark every tenth animal as the Lord’s portion.</note>, shall be a holy tithe to the Lord.

Whether the tenth animal was good or blemished, it had to be given, and one was not permitted to exchange it for another animal. Leviticus 27:33 <note>The original text incorrectly cites this as verse 35.</note>.

> One shall not inquire whether it be good or bad, nor shall it be exchanged.

But if someone did not wish to bring such an unfit animal, but rather preferred to bring a good and fit one in its place to the Temple of the Lord, then he had to bring both at the same time—the fit and the unfit. Leviticus 27:33.

> But if anyone exchanges it, then both shall be holy and shall not be redeemed.

If an Israelite did not wish to give the assigned tenth part of his land-crops, he was certainly permitted to replace it with other fruit, but he had to give somewhat more than the tenth part amounted to. Leviticus 27:31.

> If a man wishes to redeem his tithe, he shall add a fifth part to it.

All tithes from the Promised Land had to be brought to Jerusalem. Deuteronomy 12:5, 6.

> To the place which the Lord your God shall choose out of all your tribes to put His name there and make it His dwelling, you shall seek and come thither, and bring your burnt offerings and your tithes.

The Pharisee made it a special merit <note>original: "meritum." Referring to the theological concept of earning credit or favor with God.</note> that he had correctly paid his tithes on everything, from the greatest to the smallest, so that everyone had to grant him such fame, and all the priests and Levites would be his witnesses in this; which he presented in the words of Luke 18:12.

> I give tithes of all that I possess.

When they secretly with- <note>original: "ent- [zogen]" implying "withheld." The sentence likely continues on the next page regarding those who withheld their tithes.</note>

294) The Chamber of the Captain who was appointed over the incense, to ensure it was prepared correctly.

295) Chamber of the Captain of the craft of spinning, multiplying threads, dyeing with color, weaving, and decorating veils <note>original Latin: *Conclave Præfecti artificii nendi, fila multiplicandi, colore tingendi, texendi, & vela pingendi*</note>, Chamber of the one appointed over the craftsmen of the work of the incense <note>original Hebrew: *Lishkat ha-Memuneh al-omnei mlechet Ketoret*</note> <note>The Hebrew text here refers to the incense-makers mentioned in item 294, despite being placed within the entry for weavers and veil-makers.</note>, who had the tapestries under his supervision and had everything belonging to them manufactured.

296) Chamber of the Captain of the work of the priestly garments <note>original Latin: *Conclave Præfecti operis vestium sacerdotalium*</note>, who took care that the holy official garments of the High Priest and the other priests were acquired, kept in good order, and preserved.

<meta>This page continues the catalog of specific administrative and storage chambers (conclaves) within the Temple complex, specifically those located in the outer courts or surrounding structures, continuing the numbering from the previous page's list of temple officials and their respective offices.</meta>

# Model

299) **Chamber of the Men of the Station** <note>original Latin: *Conclave Virorum stationis*</note>, or of the 24 Standing Men <note>original Hebrew: *Lishkat Anshei Ha-Ma’amad*</note>? These were individuals who arrived here the day before their service in the Temple was to begin and remained so that they would be immediately present when the early morning **Divine** service commenced. <note>The "Standing Men" were representatives of the people who attended the daily sacrifices to represent the nation of Israel.</note>

300) **Chamber of the Shields** <note>original Latin: *Conclave Scutorum*</note> <note>original Hebrew: *Lishkat Ha-Magonnim*</note>, the armory, in which various kinds of weapons were kept to defend themselves in times of war.

301) **Chamber of Devoted Things** <note>original Latin: *Conclave rerum deuotarum*</note> <note>original Hebrew: *Lishkat Ha-Charamim*</note>, in which everything was placed that was consecrated or proscribed to **God** the **Lord**.

302) **Chamber of the Curtains** <note>original Latin: *Conclave velorum*</note> <note>original Hebrew: *Lishkat Ha-Parokhot*</note>, in which the veils and curtains were kept.

303) **Chamber of Worn-out Priestly Garments** <note>original Latin: *Conclave vestium sacerdotalium inueteratarum*</note> <note>original Hebrew: *Lishkat Kehunah Ha-Yeshanim*</note>, to which the discarded clothes of the priests were brought; the threads from these were used to make wicks for the golden lampstands.

304) **Chamber of Medicines** <note>original Latin: *Conclave medicamentorum*</note> <note>original Hebrew: *Lishkat Ha-Terufot*</note>, the medicine room or apothecary, in which the most precious medicines were found prepared. <note>The priests often suffered from ailments due to walking barefoot on the cold stone and wearing thin linen.</note>

305) **Chamber of Woods** <note>original Latin: *Conclave lignorum*</note>, in which the wood that had been selected by the priests and found fit for the Altar was stored.

306) **The Chamber of Palm Branches** <note>original Hebrew: *Lishkat Ha-Lulavim*</note>, into which the palm branches of the elders of the people were placed the day before the Feast of Tabernacles.

307) **Chamber of the First Fruits** <note>original Latin: *Conclave primitiarum*</note>, the room for the firstlings, in which the first fruits from the fields and gardens were stored. For the first fruits were given from seven types of produce: wheat, barley, figs, pomegranates, olives, grapes, and dates.

308) **Chamber of the Firstborn** <note>original Latin: *Conclave primogenitorum*</note> <note>original Hebrew: *Lishkat Ha-Bekhorim*</note>, the chamber for firstborn sacrificial animals, which were first brought here to the Temple and then taken through the Firstborn Gate into the inner court, where they were slaughtered on the southern side.

309) **The Gift Chamber** <note>original Hebrew: *Lishkat Nidvat Ha-Kelim*</note>, into which charitable persons brought and deposited whatever they wished to donate to the Temple.

310) **The Improvement Chamber**, in which everything given for the repair and improvement of the Temple was kept and safeguarded.

311) The chamber in which pious <term>Israelites</term> <note>The people of Israel, specifically the laypeople distinguished here from the priests and Levites.</note> deposited alms for the upbringing of poor children. <note>original Hebrew: לִשְׁכַּת חֲשָׁאִים, *Lishkat Chashaim*, or the "Chamber of the Silent," where donations were made anonymously to protect the dignity of the poor.</note>

312) The chamber in which the 93 golden vessels used in the daily **Divine** service were kept. <note>original Hebrew: לִשְׁכַּת הַכֵּלִים, *Lishkat Ha-Kelim*, the Chamber of the Vessels.</note>

313) Those chambers which contained the beautiful tapestries used to hang upon and decorate the walls of the Temple halls during high festivals.

314) The dining halls of the <term>Israelites</term>, where they were required to eat from their thank offerings. <note>Many sacrifices, such as the Peace or Thank offering, included a portion that was eaten by the person who offered it as a communal meal.</note>

315) The dining halls for the women who had brought thank offerings.

316-325) The chambers of the priests, in which they resided during their time of service and attendance in the Temple.

326-329) Those chambers of the priests in which they stored the tithes they received from the <term>Levites</term>. <note>The Levites assisted the priests; they received tithes from the general population and then gave a "tithe of the tithe" to the priests.</note> <note>original Hebrew: לִשְׁכַּת מַעְשַׂר הַכֹּהֲנִים, *Lishkat Ma’asar Ha-Kohanim*, the Chamber of the Priests' Tithe.</note>

330) A golden **Table of Showbread** <note>A sacred table that stood in the Holy Place of the Tabernacle and later the Temple, holding twelve loaves of bread representing the twelve tribes of Israel.</note> shown in a somewhat larger illustration, so that every part may be seen more clearly. Exodus 25:23.

331) The top surface of the same.

332) The golden molding around it, which was situated at the bottom of the top surface and did not extend above it. This was because the bottommost **showbread** rested directly on the table itself and, due to their length, extended beyond the edges of the table. Exodus 25:24–25.

333) The four feet of the **Table of Showbread**, which were round in their lower half and square in their upper half. Attached to these were the golden rings holding the poles for carrying. Exodus 25:26–27.

334) The framework beside and above the table, with pointed feet and the corresponding rods upon which the **showbread** were placed.

335) The 12 **loaves of showbread** in two stacks, six always atop one another. Each loaf was 6 handbreadths long, 5 handbreadths wide, and one finger thick, placed lengthwise across the width of the table.

336) The golden dishes or bowls in which the **showbread** lay.

337) The two golden incense bowls placed between the two stacks of **showbread**. <note>original Hebrew: שְׁנֵי בָזִיכֵי לְבוֹנָה, *shenei vazichei levonah*; original Latin: *duæ acerræ thuris*, meaning two incense vessels for frankincense.</note>

# Of Solomon's Temple.

### 352) The High Priest in his Vestments. 
<term>The High Priest <note>original Hebrew: כֹּהֵן הַגָּדוֹל, Kohen ha-Gadol</note></term> was the supreme authority among all priests and oversaw the entire Levite service of God. Aaron was the first High Priest, and all following him had to be from the tribe of Levi and, within that tribe, from the house and lineage of Aaron. During the inauguration of the High Priest, the sacred garments were placed upon him. *Exodus 29:5.* You shall take the holy garments and clothe Aaron. He was also anointed with the holy oil. *verse 7.* And you shall take the anointing oil, and pour it on his head and anoint him. Likewise, a sacrifice was offered for him. *verses 19, 20, 33.* You shall take the other ram, and Aaron and his sons shall lay their hands on its head, and you shall slaughter it. For the atonement has been made thereby, to <term>fill their hands <note>A Hebrew idiom for ordination or consecration to a priestly office</note></term>, that they may be sanctified. The inauguration of the High Priest lasted seven days. *Exodus 29:35.* You shall do to Aaron and his sons all that I have commanded you; seven days you shall fill their hands. From his inauguration onward, he had to have a daily grain offering sacrificed, morning and evening, namely wheat flour mixed with oil, baked in a pan, broken into twelve pieces, and a handful of incense placed upon it. When the High Priest was engaged in his high-priestly duties in the <term>Tabernacle <note>German: Stifts-Hütten; literally "Tent of Meeting"</note></term> or later in the Temple, he had to go with his head covered, and in cases of mourning or death, he was not permitted to tear his clothing, as was otherwise customary. Aaron had to observe this even during the sudden death of his two sons, Nadab and Abihu. *Leviticus 10:6.* Then Moses said to Aaron and to his sons Eleazar and Ithamar: You shall not uncover your heads nor tear your clothes, so that you do not die. And **GOD** later ordained this as a general law, which He gave to all High Priests. *Leviticus 21:10.* He who is the High Priest among his brothers, on whose head the anointing oil was poured and whose hand was filled so that he might be clothed with the garments, shall not uncover his head nor tear his clothes. He was also not permitted to approach any dead person or touch them, even if it were one of his closest relatives. *Leviticus 21:11.* He shall not go near any dead body nor defile himself, even for his father or his mother.

# Model

He was permitted to follow the corpse of his closest relative only as far as the city-gate. In important cases, one had to seek counsel from **GOD** through the High Priest. Such occurred particularly often in the time of *Joshua*. *Num. 27, 21.* And *Joshua* shall stand before the priest *Eleazar*, who shall seek counsel for him through the <term>Way of Light <note>original German: "Weise des Lichts"; a reference to the Urim and Thummim, sacred lots used by the High Priest to determine God's will</note></term> before the **LORD**. According to his word, they shall go out and come in, both he and all the children of *Israel* with him, and the whole congregation. 

When the High Priest died, he was mourned by the whole people for thirty days, just as *Aaron* was formerly mourned for just as many days by the whole congregation of *Israel* after his death. *Num. 20, 28. 29.* And *Aaron* died there on the top of the mountain; and when the whole congregation saw that *Aaron* was gone, the whole house of *Israel* wept for him thirty days. However, the High Priest had to be without any physical blemish. This was a prefiguration of *Christ's* perfect holiness. *Heb. 7, 26. 27.* For such a High Priest was fitting for us, who would be holy, innocent, undefiled, separated from sinners, and higher than the heavens; who would not need daily, like those High Priests, to first offer up sacrifices for his own sins, and then for the sins of the people. 

On the <term>Day of Atonement <note>original: "Versöhnungs-Fest," or Yom Kippur</note></term>, the High Priest went into the <term>Holy of Holies <note>The innermost chamber of the Temple, where the Ark of the Covenant was kept</note></term> to atone for the people: but *Christ* has not entered into the holy place made with hands (which is a copy of the true one), but into heaven itself, now to appear before the face of **GOD** for us. *Heb. 9, 24.* Through the High Priest, **GOD** spoke and gave answers to those who sought counsel from **GOD**: And through *Christ*, **GOD** speaks with us, who has revealed to us all the counsel of **GOD** regarding our salvation. *Heb. 1, 1.* After **GOD** at various times and in various manners spoke in time past to the fathers through the prophets, He has in these last days spoken to us through the Son. 

When the High Priest went into the **Holy of Holies** on the **Day of Atonement**, no person was permitted to be present in the entire temple; this indicated that *Christ*, the only true High Priest, alone would accomplish the work of redemption and reconciliation with **GOD**. *Acts 4, 12.* There is salvation in no other, nor is there any other name given to men by which we must be saved, but in the name of **Jesus Christ**. When the High Priest died, he who had committed unintentional manslaughter and had fled to the <term>City of Refuge <note>Cities designated as safe havens for those who killed someone accidentally</note></term> was permitted to return to his house and prop-

361) The girdle with which this white tunic was bound. v. 39.

Within it, precious stones were set in gold like small crowns and fastened so that they could not fall out. The LORD God commanded Moses to make this breastplate of office thus: Exod. 28, 15. 16. You shall make the breastplate of office with the same skill as the Ephod; of gold, blue silk, scarlet, rose-red <note>original: "Rosinroth"</note>, and twisted white silk; it shall be square and doubled, a span wide...

7. a **Lyncurius** <note>A stone often identified in the ancient world as amber or a yellow-hued zircon.</note> <note>original: לֶשֶׁם, *Lyncurius*</note>, in which was the name of the tribe of Gad.

9. an **Amethyst** <note>original: אַחְלָמָה, *Amethystus*</note>, in which was the name Issachar.
In the fourth row was:
10. a **Turquoise** <note>The Latin *Berillus thalassius* suggests a sea-green beryl.</note> <note>original: תַּרְשִׁישׁ, *Berillus thalassius*</note>, in which was the name of the tribe of Zebulon.

<meta>This page continues the catalog of the twelve gemstones on the High Priest’s breastplate and begins a theological explanation of how the Temple and its rituals serve as "types" or foreshadowings of Christ and the Christian life.</meta>

-># Model<-

11. an Onyx <note>original Hebrew: שׁהם (*shoham*)</note>, Onyx, in which was the name Manasseh.
12. a Jasper <note>original Hebrew: ישׁפה (*yashfeh*)</note> Jasper, in which the name of the tribe of Ephraim was engraved.

The High Priest was required to carry these 12 names of all the tribes of Israel upon his breast, to signify that the one true High Priest, **Jesus Christ**, would constantly carry the memory of His believers in His heart. John 10:14, Chapter 13:1.

Thus, in the entire Levitical service, everything was hieroglyphic <note>In this context, meaning symbolic or encoded with sacred meaning</note> and emblematic, where the future was represented through nothing but symbols. For there is no part of the Temple, nor any circumstance or ceremony, that did not have a view toward Christ and His Christians in the New Testament. For just as **God** dwelt in the pillar of cloud in the Holy of Holies <note>The innermost, most sacred chamber of the Temple</note> of the Temple: so in **Jesus** Christ the entire fullness of the **Godhead** was to dwell bodily. *Colossians 2:9.* Just as the first Temple was indeed destroyed and torn down, but built up again after the redemption from the Babylonian captivity was accomplished: so Christ’s body was to be laid down through death, but after the redemption of the human race was fulfilled, it was to be raised up again upon His resurrection from the dead. Of this He says in *John 2:19*: "Destroy this temple, and in three days I will raise it up again." *verse 21.* But He was speaking of the temple of His body. The Apostle Paul unfolds <note>original Latin: *evolviret*</note> the prefiguration <note>original Latin: *typum*; a "type" is a biblical person or event that foreshadows a future reality</note> of the Temple, insofar as it prefigured Christians. *1 Corinthians 3:16.* "Do you not know that you are **God’s** temple, and that the Spirit of **God** dwells in you," just as **God** formerly dwelt in the Holy of Holies and revealed His presence there. Just as the Temple was also founded upon an immovable, great rock that was 500 cubits long and wide, so the Church and all believers within it should be founded upon the Rock of Salvation—upon the true, tested cornerstone, **Jesus** Christ. *Matthew 16:18, 1 Corinthians 3:11.* Just as the Temple was most magnificently adorned on the inside, so the believers of **God** are inwardly decorated with the most magnificent gifts of the Holy Spirit. *Galatians 5:22, Psalm 45:14.* In the Temple, after the sacrificial service, God’s Word was contemplated in the House of Learning <note>A reference to the *Beth Midrash* or places of study associated with the Temple complex</note>: thus the Holy Spirit teaches Christians **God’s** Word and leads them into all truth. *John 16:13, Chapter 14:26.* In the Temple 
wer- <note>This is a catchword, the first part of the word "were" or "became" (German: *wurden*), which continues on the following page.</note>

<meta>This page continues the theological explanation of the Temple of Solomon as a "type" or symbolic foreshadowing of the New Testament church and the inner spiritual life of the individual believer.</meta>

### of Solomon's Temple.

diligent and devout prayer was offered: Likewise, the Holy Spirit, who is a Spirit of grace and prayer, calls out in the hearts of the believers *Abba* <note>original Hebrew/Aramaic: אבא (*abba*); an intimate term for "Father"</note>, dear Father, and intercedes for them with wordless sighs. *Romans* 8:15, 26. In the Temple, daily sacrifices were offered to **God**, and Christians are to offer their bodies and souls as a sacrifice that is living, holy, and pleasing to **God**. *Romans* 12:1. In the Temple, the Psalms of David were sung and **God** was daily lauded and praised. Similarly, faithful Christians sing and make music to the Lord in their hearts. *Ephesians* 5:20 <note>In modern Bibles, this is usually Ephesians 5:19</note>. In the Holy of Holies <note>the *Allerheiligsten*, the innermost and most sacred chamber of the Temple</note> of the Temple, the majestic **God** dwelt in the pillar of cloud and fire; and in the temple of the heart of the faithful, the eternal and living **God** dwells with His grace, of which the Holy Apostle Paul assures them, saying: You are the temple of the living **God**, just as **God** says: I will dwell in them and walk among them, and I will be their **God**, and they shall be my people. *2 Corinthians* 6:16. The entire assembly of such a great and countless multitude in the Temple—where many thousands of Israelites gathered together on high festivals, including many strangers arriving from far-distant places—was a prefiguration <note>a "type" or symbolic preview of things to come</note> of eternal life, where the faithful from all places in the world come together to praise **God**. John, in his Revelation, saw a great multitude from all nations, peoples, languages, and tongues, who all with a single voice praised **God** and cried out: *Amen* / Blessing and glory and wisdom and thanksgiving and honor and power and strength be unto our God forever and ever /
->*Amen. Revelation* 7:9, 12.<-
